# Decisão da Comissão, de 22/04/2026 relativa à compatibilidade com o mercado comum de uma operação de concentração (Processo no COMP/M.12076 - RTL / SKY DACH) com base no Regulamento (CE) n. 139/2004 do Conselho (Apenas faz fé o texto em língua inglesa)

**Texto:** versão original publicada no Jornal Oficial (sem alterações posteriores, se as houver).

Subject:   Case M.12076 – RTL / SKY DACH
           Commission decision pursuant to Article 6(1)(b) of Council Regulation
           No 139/2004 (1) and Article 57 of the Agreement on the European
           Economic Area (2)



Dear Sir or Madam,


(1)    On 27 February 2026, the European Commission received notification of a
       proposed concentration pursuant to Article 4 of the Merger Regulation by which
       RTL Deutschland GmbH, Germany (‘RTL’, or ‘Notifying Party’), controlled by
       Bertelsmann SE & Co. KGaA, will acquire within the meaning of Article 3(1)(b)
       of the Merger Regulation sole control of Sky German Holdings GmbH, Germany
       (‘Sky DACH’, Germany) (the ‘Transaction’). (3) RTL and Sky DACH are
       designated hereinafter as the ‘Parties’.



(1)   OJ L 24, 29.1.2004, p. 1 (the ‘Merger Regulation’). With effect from 1 December 2009, the Treaty

      replacement of ‘Community’ by ‘Union’ and ‘common market’ by ‘internal market’. The
      terminology of the TFEU will be used throughout this decision.

(2)     On 27 March 2026, after the Commission informed the Parties that it could not
        be excluded that the Transaction would raise serious doubts as to its compatibility
        with the internal market, the Notifying Party submitted commitments pursuant to
        Article 6(2) of the Merger Regulation with a view to removing these possible
        serious doubts.
(3)     However, upon further investigation and following the submission of additional
        information and evidence by the Parties, (4) the Commission concluded that the
        Transaction would not raise serious doubts as further explained in this Decision.
        After being informed accordingly, the Notifying Party withdrew the
        commitments. Against this background, in line with paragraph 84 of the
        Commission notice on remedies, neither are the commitments submitted by the
        Notifying Party assessed in this Decision, nor is the authorisation of the
        Transaction conditional upon compliance with the proposed commitments.


1.      THE PARTIES
(4)     RTL is an entertainment group based in Germany and active across a broad
        spectrum of media such as TV and streaming, print and digital, radio and podcasts.
        RTL, as part of RTL Group, is indirectly solely controlled by Bertelsmann SE &
        Co. KGaA (Germany). RTL’ Group is active in the entire European Economic
        Area (‘EEA’), but its main activities relating to TV and online entertainment
        along with production and broadcasting services are mainly in Germany and
        Austria. In 2024, RTL reported total revenues of approximately EUR 18 988
        million of which approximately EUR […] million was EU-wide turnover.
(5)     Sky DACH consists of the audiovisual business currently run by Sky German
        Holdings GmbH, controlled by Comcast Corporation (‘Comcast’, United States).
        Sky DACH is active in the supply of linear and non-linear audiovisual (‘AV’)
        programming via set-top-boxes and streaming services to consumers on a
        subscription basis, the licensing and acquisition of AV content, the creation of
        entertainment and sports-centred channels, the wholesale supply and acquisition
        of channels and AV services, and the sale of advertising space on its channels and
        services. In the EEA, Sky DACH is active in Germany, Austria as well as in
        certain other German-speaking countries/regions (Trentino Alto-Adige/South
        Tyrol in Italy, Luxembourg and Liechtenstein). In 2024, Sky DACH reported total
        revenues of approximately EUR […] million of which approximately EUR […]
        million was EU-wide turnover.


2.      THE TRANSACTION

2.1.    Concentration
(6)     The Transaction concerns the acquisition by RTL of sole control over Sky DACH.


(4)    Including Notifying Party submission and economic analysis of 24 March 2026 (“Follow up
       submission to SoP” and Annalect’s Media Planning Analysis),and Notifying Party’s responses to
       Commission’s requests to information (“RFI”) dated 25 March 2026 (RFI 15 response),
       26 March 2026 (RFI 16 response), 27 March 2026 (RFI 17 response), 30 March 2026 (RFI 18
       response), 7 and 8 April 2026 (RFI 19 response), 9 and 11 April 2026 (RFI 20 response), 13 April
       2026 (RFI 21 response), 15 April 2026 (RFI 22 response), 17 April 2026 (RFI 23 response), 19 and
       20 April 2026 (RFI 24 response), 20 April 2026 (RFI 25 response), 19 April 2026 (RFI 26 response),
       amongst others.
                                                   2
(7)     Pursuant to a Framework Agreement dated 26 June 2025, RTL will acquire 100%
        of the shareholding of Sky DACH from Comcast for EUR 150 million in cash and
        up to EUR 377 million in deferred variable consideration. Post-Transaction, RTL
        will hold 100% of the shares in Sky DACH.
(8)     Therefore, the Transaction constitutes a concentration within the meaning of
        Article 3(1)(b) of the Merger Regulation.

2.2.    Transaction rationale
(9)     The stated rationale for the Transaction is to enable RTL to address changes in
        media consumption throughout Europe. In particular, the Transaction would help
        RTL to grow its offer of subscription streaming in order to cater for the growing
        demand for online video, as well as video-on-demand (‘VoD’) streaming and the
        diminishing popularity of free-to-air (‘FTA’) TV. According to RTL, this would
        allow RTL to compete better with leading AV services provided by global players
        such as Amazon Prime Video, Netflix, and Disney+.

2.3.    Union Dimension
(10)    The undertakings concerned have a combined aggregate world-wide turnover of
        more than EUR 5 000 million (EUR […] million) and each of RTL and Sky
        DACH have an aggregate EU-wide turnover of more than EUR 250 million (RTL:
        EUR […] million; Sky DACH: EUR […] million). While Sky DACH achieves
        more than two thirds of its aggregate EU-wide turnover within one and the same
        Member State (Germany), this is not the case for RTL. (5) The notified operation
        therefore has a Union dimension.


3.      INDUSTRY OVERVIEW

3.1.    Introduction
(11)    The Parties are both active across the AV value chain. The AV value chain
        comprises the supply and acquisition of AV content (upstream), the wholesale
        supply and acquisition of TV channels and AV services, alongside the wholesale
        supply of TV signal transmission services (intermediate), and the retail provision
        of AV services to end users (downstream). In addition, some AV services operate
        on an ad-supported basis within two-sided markets, where providers supply
        advertising space alongside content. The Parties are both active in the supply of
        advertising space in AV content. (6)
(12)    The AV value chain has been undergoing a significant change over the last years,
        due to the growth of streaming platforms, most notably the main global
        Subscription-based Video-on-Demand players (‘SVoD’) (Netflix, Amazon and
        Disney), as well as YouTube, which have been changing media consumption
        patterns and resulting in a decline in linear TV consumption. (7) Such changes
        have had impact at the several levels of the AV value chain as well as in
        advertising.




(5)    Turnover calculated in accordance with Article 5 of the Merger Regulation.
(6)    Form CO, paras 21-22.
(7)    European media industry outlook, pages 41 and 73.

3.2.      Supply and acquisition of AV content
(13)      The markets for AV content constitute the upstream level of the AV supply chain,
          encompassing both the production of AV content and the licensing of distribution
          rights to wholesale suppliers of TV channels and AV services, as well as retail
          providers of AV services. At this level of the AV value chain, production studios
          either produce AV content commissioned by certain market participants for the
          use on their own AV service or license AV content which is available off-the-
          shelf (pre-produced content). This includes the licensing of non-sports content
          such as films and series, as well as the licensing of sports media rights for sport
          events. (8)
(14)      Both ‘originals’ and ‘acquired’ titles in the portfolio of broadcasters and streamers
          are important for the audiovisual content market. Investments in originals ensure
          that new productions will take place, whereas acquisitions provide opportunities
          for rights holders to monetise already existing works (9).
(15)      Third-party customers are typically (i) TV channel suppliers (TV broadcasters),
          which then incorporate the AV content into linear TV channels, or (ii)
          content/streaming platform operators (e.g., over-the-top platforms, VoD, pay-per
          view), which then retail the AV content to end users on a non-linear basis. Linear
          content refers to AV content distributed at a scheduled time through TV channels
          and has typically been how traditional TV broadcasters (pay-TV and FTA) have
          made content available to consumers. Non-linear content refers to AV content
          made available not on a scheduled basis, for instance content made available on
          demand to consumers by content platform operators 24/7 via OTT streaming.
(16)      Spending in the production and acquisition of AV content has been increasing
          over the last decade, covering all types of content, including films, series and non-
          scripted formats, which has allowed the diversification of the offerings of
          broadcasters and streamers. (10) In addition, streaming platforms have been
          increasingly moving into sports content, which had been traditionally dominated
          by broadcasts, as a driver for large audiences. Streaming platform have thus
          contributed to a more globalised consumption of sports, with European
          competitions, such as the German Bundesliga or the Spanish La Liga, being
          available in the US, and US sports and competitions being available and growing
          in popularity in the EU. (11)

3.3.      Wholesale supply of TV signal transmission services, TV channels and AV
          services

3.3.1.    Wholesale supply of TV signal transmission services
(17)      TV signal transmission chain includes signal generation, processing, broadcasting
          and reception, ensuring quality and reliability during the process. These elements
          are aligned together to ensure effective broadcasting and viewer experience.
(18)      The wholesale of TV signal transmission services is an adjacent activity to the
          wholesale of TV channels and AV services. TV broadcasters, on the demand side,




(8)      Form CO, para 233.
(9)      European media industry outlook, page 63.
(10)     European media industry outlook, pages 59 to 64.
(11)     European media industry outlook, page 64.
                                                   4
          negotiate the terms and conditions of the transmission of the TV signal for their
          channels with the infrastructure operators, which are on the supply side. (12)

3.3.2.    Wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels and AV services
(19)      The wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels and AV services constitutes
          the intermediate level between the upstream creation and licensing of content and
          the downstream retail distribution of AV content and services to end customers.
          At the wholesale level, undertakings acquire rights from rights holders and
          aggregate this content mainly into linear TV channels and SVoD services. Apart
          from the possibility to offer these products direct-to-consumer (‘DtC’) at the retail
          level of the value chain, these wholesale products are supplied to downstream
          providers of AV retail services) for retail distribution to end customers. (13)
(20)      Providers of AV retail services offer their customers individual TV channels/AV
          services or a catalogue of various TV channels/AV services, which can either be
          their own TV channels/AV services or procured from third parties. For this
          purpose, providers of TV channels/AV services (such as SVoD services) enter
          into wholesale agreements (distribution agreements) and/or commission-
          based/agency model agreements with AV retailers. (14)
(21)      Under a wholesale agreement, the acquiring party purchases access to a third-
          party TV channel or AV service (such as SVoD) for its customers at a pre-
          determined price and resells it as part of its own offering. The consumers enter
          into a contractual relationship with the acquiring party (i.e. not the TV/AV service
          supplier). Hence, the acquiring party assumes full control over pricing and thus,
          also the economic risks. Under an agency agreement, third-party SVoD services
          can be integrated into an acquiring party’s offering but the acquiring party acts as
          an intermediary/platform that promotes the third-party SVoD service in exchange
          for a commission per sale on behalf and in the name of the other provider. (15)
(22)      Wholesale acquirers act as aggregators of TV channels and AV services, offering
          packages or bundled offers at retail level.

3.4.      Retail of TV channels/AV services to end users
(23)      The retail provision of AV services to end users constitutes the downstream level
          of the AV value chain which follows the upstream level for the production and
          licensing of AV content and the intermediate level for wholesale TV channels and
          AV services.
(24)      For the retail provision of AV services, undertakings can either (i) acquire
          broadcasting rights from content producers and rights holders to make this AV
          content available to end users in linear or non-linear AV services, such as SVoD
          or Advertising-Based Video on Demand (‘AVoD’) services, or (ii) acquire TV
          channels and other AV services from wholesale suppliers and offer them as a
          bundle to end users. AV retailers offer their AV services to end users either free
          of charge or against a fee. For the distribution of their services to end users, AV
          retailers use traditional networks such as cable, satellite, digital terrestrial TV

(14)     Form CO, para. 785

        (‘DTT’) or the distribution via the internet, i.e. internet protocol TV (‘IPTV’) or
        over-the-top (‘OTT’). (16)
(25)    In some instances, companies active at the retail level combine the retail offering
        of other, competing AV retail service providers with their own offering and offer
        these to end-customers through their own service platform (in particular by way
        of acquiring SVoD services at the wholesale level or acting as an
        intermediary/agent for the promotion of third-party SVoD services). (17) For the
        remainder of this Decision, such combined or bundled offerings of own and third-
        party AV services at retail level are referred to as ‘aggregation’ or ‘aggregated
        AV services’ and the service providers offering such services as ‘aggregators’.
(26)    European broadcasters are shifting to deliver both linear and on-demand content
        across all types of distribution platforms, as a response to increased pressure from
        global platforms that offer non-linear AV services (e.g. SVoD and AVoD), and
        the decrease in linear TV consumption. In 2024, all of the top 15 EU broadcasters
        offered VoD services. SVoD remains the most concentrated segment, with the top
        10 platforms in the EU holding 84% of subscriptions and 90% of revenues (18)

3.5.    AV Advertising
(27)    AV advertising is part of a two-sided market. Publishers/broadcasters produce and
        distribute AV content for their viewers and offer the corresponding advertising
        inventory (19) to customers, both media buying agencies (20) as well as directly to
        advertisers. Customers purchase airtime across suppliers according to price and
        their capability to reach relevant target groups. (21)
(28)    In the AV sector, revenues are mostly generated by consumers spending on
        content, or by advertisers paying for consumers’ exposure to their brands as a way
        of monetising users’ attention and screen time. (22).
(29)    The AV advertising sector is undergoing significant changes. There is
        competition for advertising between an increasingly diverse set of AV players,
        putting traditional TV FTA broadcasters under pressure. In Europe, as of 2023,
        online video sharing platforms, including YouTube, gained 24% of the video
        advertising market while broadcasters retained 76%. Advertising revenues of
        online video sharing platforms have been growing much faster than TV
        broadcasters’, whose advertising revenues have been declining significantly in
        real terms. Further, streaming platforms have been introducing ad-supported
        subscription-based Video on Demand (“SAVoD”) (23) while maintaining the

(20)   Media buying agencies provide services including the purchase of advertising time and/or space in
       various types of media, such as broadcast and cable TV, newspapers and magazines, radio,
       billboards and the internet, for clients running advertising campaigns (i.e., advertiser). Media buying

        prominence of SVoD. This is projected to increase streaming platforms’ revenues
        by 20%, a significant boost to overall earnings. AVoD enables targeted
        advertising while expanding audience reach through free or cheaper, ad-supported
        content models. As of 2022, streaming platforms such as Netflix, Disney+, HBO
        Max, and Paramount+ have combined subscription fees with advertising-based
        tiers. This approach allows streamers to capture a broader range of consumers,
        including price-sensitive users willing to view ads in exchange for lower
        subscription costs (e.g. Netflix has doubled its advertising revenue year-over-
        year). (24)


4.      PARTIES’ ACTIVITIES
(30)    In the EEA, both Parties are active across various levels of the AV value chain in
        Germany, Austria, Liechtenstein, Luxembourg, Trentino-Alto Adige/South Tyrol
        (Italy) (together referred to as ‘German-speaking Territories’). (25) The Parties
        are also active in the supply of advertising space in AV content in Germany and
        Austria. (26)
(31)    RTL is active across a broad spectrum of media, such as TV and streaming, print
        and digital, radio and podcasts. RTL’s main activities relate to the supply of FTA
        channels (27) and other AV services, along with production broadcasting and
        advertising services, mostly in Germany. RTL is part of the RTL Group which
        has, through Fremantle Media Group (‘Fremantle’), (28) activities in content
        production and distribution. RTL is also active in the supply of advertising space
        through various media, including TV, radio, newspapers, as well as online
        advertising. (29)
(32)    Sky is an audiovisual business active in the supply of linear and non-linear AV
        services, via set-top boxes and streaming services to consumers, the licensing and
        acquisition of AV content, the creation of entertainment and sports centred
        channels, the wholesale supply and acquisition of channels (30) and AV services,
        and the sale of advertising space on its channels and services, as well as third
        party’s advertising inventory. (31)

4.1.    Supply and acquisition of AV content
(33)    Both Parties are active in the supply and acquisition of AV content. While the
        Parties’ activities in the supply of AV content in the German-speaking Territories
        are very limited, their activities in the acquisition of AV content are more
        substantial. (32)
(34)    RTL has minor activities in the supply of commissioned and pre-produced AV
        content in the German-speaking Territories. The majority of its content portfolio
        is produced in-house for captive use, namely entertainment shows, reality shows,
        news formats, as well as selective fiction productions. RTL focuses on inhouse
        and captive productions but also commissions some non-fiction content.
        Regarding the acquisition of pre-produced AV content, RTL primarily acquires
        rights to films (e.g., Barbie, Harry Potter, James Bond) and series (e.g., Two and
        a Half Men, The Rookie, Young Sheldon), which is required for a well-rounded
        FTA TV offering and which also enables RTL’s SVoD service (i.e. RTL+) to
        offer a broad range of acquired pre-produced content with a focus on fiction.
        [Business/commercial information of RTL].
(35)    RTL also acquires pre-produced sports content, including American football (the
        National Football League) and football, where RTL owns partial FTA rights over
        a variety of leagues and cups (e.g., UEFA Europa League, UEFA Nations
        Leagues, and Bundesliga top matches). In addition, RTL sublicenses selected
        Formula 1 races from Sky DACH. These rights are primarily offered through
        RTL’s FTA channels and also live on RTL+. Some less relevant sports rights are
        available exclusively on RTL+, including Oktagon MMA. (33)
(36)    Sky DACH is not active in the supply of commissioned AV content and has only
        limited activities in the supply of pre-produced AV content. On the acquisition of
        AV content, Sky DACH focuses on sports content and high-quality entertainment
        content. Its portfolio is centred around exclusive broadcasting rights for top-tier
        football competitions such as the German Bundesliga, the Austrian Bundesliga,
        the DFB Pokal and the Premier League. In addition to football, Sky DACH also
        offers coverage of motorsports (e.g., Formula 1 and MotoGP), golf (e.g., The
        Masters Tournament and PGA Championship) and tennis (e.g., WTA/ATP Tour
        and US Open), as well as other sport disciplines such as NBA, NHL and the World
        Surf League.
(37)    Regarding entertainment content, Sky DACH focuses on the acquisition of high-
        quality fiction content, such as feature films and series with high production value
        and international relevance. For instance, Sky DACH acquires exclusive
        1st pay/SVoD window rights to show blockbuster feature films shortly after their
        theatrical and home video release (e.g., Minions, Jurassic World), as well as first-
        run rights for series, often with a premier date directly after the original US launch
        (e.g., The Rookie, House of the Dragon). Moreover, Sky DACH acquires well-
        established films and series (e.g., Rambo, Hawaii Five-O), as well as exclusive
        international Sky Original fiction formats produced by Sky Studios (e.g., Gangs
        of London, The Day of Jackal). To a lesser extent, Sky DACH also shows locally
        produced Sky Originals such as documentaries and reality TV formats. Sky
        DACH collaborates with a wide range of international and domestic licensors,
        including major US studios such as NBCUniversal, Warner Bros., Paramount,

4.2.      Wholesale supply of TV signal transmission services, TV channels and AV
          services
(38)      Both Parties are active at the wholesale level of the AV value chain, i.e., in the
          acquisition of TV signal transmission services and the acquisition and supply of
          TV channels and AV services. (35)

4.2.1.    Wholesale supply of TV signal transmission services
(39)      RTL is active in the acquisition of TV signal transmission services at the
          wholesale level in Germany, Austria and Liechtenstein. RTL’s TV channels are
          transmitted via every distribution technology available to achieve maximum
          reach. Sky DACH is active in the acquisition of TV signal transmission services
          at the wholesale level in Germany and Austria for satellite and cable transmission.
          For internet protocol television (‘IPTV’), Sky DACH does not purchase TV signal
          transmission services. RTL and Sky DACH are not active in the wholesale supply
          of TV signal transmission services. (36)

4.2.2.    Wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels and AV services
(40)      RTL is hardly active in the wholesale acquisition of TV channels and SVoD
          services as it does not generally purchase third-party products for further
          integration. The activities of RTL in the wholesale acquisition of TV channels and
          SVoD services are currently limited to the purchase of RTL ZWEI for the use on
          RTL+, and will also encompass HBO Max. (37)
(41)      Sky DACH purchases FTA and pay-TV channels for further distribution via its
          platform Sky Q (cable and satellite), Sky Stream (IPTV) and, to a limited extent,
          WOW in Germany and Austria. In addition, through wholesale agreements and
          agreements based on commission/agency models, Sky Stream and Sky Q offer
          access to various other SVoD providers, including Netflix, Disney+, Trendsports,
          DYN, Joyn, Apple TV and RTL+. (38)
(42)      With respect to the wholesale supply of TV channels and AV services, RTL
          primarily acts as a supplier of FTA and pay-TV channels (39) to satellite, cable,
          DTT and IPTV network operators within Germany, Austria, Luxembourg and
          Liechtenstein (via Switzerland). RTL offers it SVoD service RTL+ at the
          wholesale level to aggregators (i.e., to Deutsche Telekom AG (‘DTAG’) in
          Germany as well as Telekom Austria and Simpli TV in Austria) and under
          commission/agency-based agreements to various other third-parties, including

        DTAG/MagentaTV, Amazon Prime Video Channels, Telefonica and Waipu TV)
        and under commission/agency -based agreements to various other third-parties,
        including SmartTVs or gaming consoles, such as Amazon Fire TC, Apple TV,
        Microsoft X-Box, Sony PlayStation LG, Samsung, Roku, VIDAA (Hisense). (41)

4.3.    Retail of TV channels/AV services to end users
(44)    Both Parties are active in the retail provision of TV channels and AV services to
        end users.
(45)    RTL acts primarily as a wholesale supplier and not a retailer of its TV channels
        in German-speaking Territories and its presence at the retail level of the AV value
        chain is limited to its OTT service RTL+. Through RTL+, end-users can access
        RTL’s TV channels, certain live content confined to RTL+, and to a library of
        commissioned and pre-produced AV content, music, podcasts and magazines.
        The service is subscription-based and usually ad-supported. RTL also engages
        with a number of distributors as agents for the retail of RTL+. As of January 2026,
        RTL acquires Warner’s SVoD service and resells it at the retail level in a bundle
        with RTL+, but it is not an aggregator of AV services. (42)
(46)    Sky DACH primarily pursues a DtC approach and acts as a retail supplier of
        subscription-based paid TV channels and SVoD services in the German-speaking
        Territories. Sky DACH retails its own pay-TV offering and SVoD offering
        (WOW in Germany and Sky X in Austria). The offering entails linear pay-TV
        channels, third party linear FTA and pay-TV channels as well as its own and third
        party VoD programming, which Sky aggregates on its own platform for its
        subscribers. It uses satellite, cable and IPTV for its pay-TV offering and OTT for
        its SVoD offering. Sky DACH also supplies its pay-TV offering to commercial
        premises. (43)

4.4.    AV advertising
(47)    Both Parties are active in the supply of advertising inventory. In the EEA, the
        Parties’ activities overlap in Germany and Austria. The Parties are not active in
        online search advertising. (44)
(48)    RTL sells advertising space on TV, SAVoD, Addressable TV (‘ATV’) (45) and
        online video in Germany via its wholly-owned subsidiary AdAlliance.
        AdAlliance’s sales are predominantly to media buying agencies and to a lesser
        extent to direct customers (advertisers). In Austria, RTL sells its inventory via

        intermediated sales, selling third-party inventory in digital, print and audio.
        Regarding TV, AdAlliance is only selling inventory of channels which are owned
        and operated by RTL. (47)
(49)    In Germany, AdAlliance sells advertising in digital, print and audio for third
        parties mainly as a wholesaler/reseller, selling inventory in AdAlliance’s own
        name and on its own account. In particular, AdAlliance conducts intermediated
        sales of SAVoD ad space for RTL2. RTL does not resort to third-party sales
        houses in Germany to sell its own advertising space, but it partners with other
        German sales houses to enhance the utilization of its online-video (in-stream)
        inventory. (48)
(50)    RTL sells online video inventory in Germany via AdAlliance. In this context,
        AdAlliance conducts direct sales with respect to inventory owned by RTL and
        intermediated sales with respect to inventory of third parties. (49)
(51)    In Austria, RAdA sells Sky Sport Austria’s linear TV inventory as well as digital
        advertising on sky.at. In addition, RAdA conducts intermediated sales of TV
        advertising space for Canal+, Oe24.TV, Sport 1, Warner, ORF (for advertisers
        outside of Austria), Krone.TV and R9 Oesterreich. (50)
(52)    RTL is also active in the supply of advertising technology. It owns and operates
        smartX which is an adserver and a supply-side platform for the programmatic sale
        of AV advertising inventory. (51)
(53)    Sky DACH markets its inventory in TV, online video and SAVoD via Sky Media.
        Sky Media sells to both media buying agencies and direct customers in roughly
        equal amounts. Sky DACH’s TV and VoD ad sales heavily depend on its current
        Bundesliga inventory. Customers typically book airtime on Sky DACH inventory
        to reach very specific target groups, often related to premium sports content. A
        significant share of sales is made directly to advertisers from the bookmaking
        industry. (52)
(54)    Sky DACH has no own FTA inventory. Its activity in FTA advertising in
        Germany mainly relates to sales of advertising space owned by High View, an
        operator of small FTA channels for music videos. In Germany, Sky DACH
        conducts intermediated sales of third-party pay-TV inventory, for High View,
        Hearst Networks Germany and Mainstream Media. Sky DACH also has a limited
        business of intermediated SAVoD sales for third parties, again for High View,
        Hearst Networks Germany and Mainstream Media, as well as for Warner. (53)
(55)    In Austria Sky DACH makes sales only indirectly via agencies. Sky DACH uses
        RAdA as a sales house for TV in Austria, Content Performance Group GmbH
        (‘COPE’) for online video inventory and P7S1 for SAVoD inventory. (54)

5.        MARKET DEFINITION

5.1.      Market for the supply and acquisition of AV content
(57)      The upstream level of the value chain concerns the acquisition of AV content for
          TV channels and other AV services (in particular SVoD services) and the supply
          of AV content to third-party customers, typically (i) TV channel suppliers (TV
          broadcasters), which then incorporate the AV content into linear TV channels, or
          (ii) content/streaming platform operators (e.g., over-the-top platforms, video on
          demand, pay-per view), which then retail the AV content to end users on a non-
          linear basis.
(58)      Both Parties are active at the upstream level of the value chain, with a substantial
          overlap in the acquisition of AV content. RTL has minor activities in the supply
          of commissioned and pre-produced AV content in the German-speaking
          Territories, while Sky DACH is not active in the supply of commissioned AV
          content, engaging in the supply of pre-produced AV content to a limited extent
          (mostly related to sub-licensing of content). Regarding the acquisition of AV
          content, RTL focuses on inhouse and captive productions but also commissions
          some non-fiction content. Further, RTL acquires a broad spectrum of (non-
          premium) fiction and non-fiction AV content off-the-shelf and in the acquisition
          of mostly non-premium sports rights. Sky DACH focuses on the acquisition of
          premium pre-produced entertainment and sports content. (56)

5.1.1.    Product market definition

5.1.1.1. Commission precedents
(59)      The Commission has previously considered there are separate markets for: (i) the
          production and supply of commissioned AV content (i.e. new content produced
          on request), and (ii) the licensing of broadcasting rights for pre-produced AV
          content (off-the-shelf content). (57)
(60)      With regard to the market for production of commissioned AV content, the
          Commission found the product market to be limited to non-captive AV
          production, thereby excluding captive AV production (i.e., AV content produced
          by broadcasters for use on their own TV channels), as this AV content is not
          offered on the market. (58)

(61)      For both commissioned as well as pre-produced AV content, the Commission
          has considered, but ultimately left open, numerous further segmentations,
          including: (i) segmenting by content type (i.e., films, sports, and other AV
          content), (ii) by exhibition window (SVoD, transactional video on demand

        (‘TVoD’), pay per view (‘PPV’), and FTA). (59) The Commission also considered
        a potential distinction between the acquisition of content to be incorporated into
        linear TV channels and non-linear AV services (i.e., SVoD, TVoD and PPV), but
        ultimately left the market definition open.
(62)    Within certain content types, the Commission considered some further
        segmentation. In what concerns films, the Commission considered distinguishing
        between US and non-US films, premium and non-premium content and by
        exhibition window, as identified above but further distinguishing between first
        pay-TV window and second pay-TV window. (60)
(63)    With regards to other AV content, the Commission has considered a distinction
        between scripted and non-scripted (with scripted AV content being produced on
        an existing script, story or character, while non-scripted AV content is content not
        based on a script, for example game shows, reality shows and talent shows), as
        well as a potential segmentation by genre (drama, comedy, documentaries, kids’
        programs, reality shows, talk shows, talent shows, factual entertainment, etc.). (61)
(64)    Within sports content, in the recent decision in Liberty Media/Dorna, the
        Commission has excluded a possible segmentation of the market by type of sport,
        namely in what concerned a potential market for motorsports content only,
        without taking a view on a potential football market, (62) separate from other
        sports. (63) The Commission also considered that the market for the licensing of
        broadcasting rights of all sports content may be further segmented into regular
        (i.e., sport events scheduled on a yearly/seasonal basis) and irregular sports
        content (i.e., sport events that only occur on a non-yearly basis, for instance every
        two years), as well as premium and non-premium sports content (based on an
        analysis of significant gaps of annualized fee value of sports rights at national
        level. (64) The Commission found that possible segmentations between FTA and
        pay-TV and integrating into linear and non-linear AV services were not
        relevant. (65)

5.1.1.2. The Notifying Party’s view
(65)    The Notifying Party submits that the relevant market should encompass both
        commissioned and pre-produced AV content, without further segmentation.

                                                  13
(66)    In what concerns commissioned AV content, the Notifying Party submits it is
        neither necessary nor appropriate to segment the market further by content type
        or by genre. The various content types and genres are substitutable from a supply
        and demand-side perspective, as TV broadcasters and streaming services acquire
        a wide variety of AV content from different sources and formats, designed to
        target the same audience, and production studios can start producing other AV
        content or genre within a short timeframe and without significant additional
        costs. (66)
(67)    With regards to pre-produced AV content, the Notifying Party considers that the
        market might, but does not need to be, further sub-segmented according to the
        type of content. The Notifying Party claims that sports and non-sports content can
        be equally appealing to the viewer and therefore be considered substitutable and
        assume there is often one and the same target group to be reached across sports
        and non-sports content AV offerings. Furthermore, there is supply-side
        substitutability, specifically within non-sports content, as a company only active
        in the production of one type of content (e.g. films), would be able to start
        producing other AV content (e.g. series) within a short timeframe and without
        incurring significant additional costs. (67)
(68)    Furthermore, the Notifying Party does not consider it appropriate to segment
        further between premium and non-premium AV content, in view of difficulties
        regarding the definition of what constitutes “premium” AV content, or between
        US films and non-US films, given that such types compete to attract the same
        viewing audience in the EEA, whilst major US studios have been also producing
        content in the EU. (68) Specifically in relation to other AV content (non-sports and
        non-films), the Notifying Party does not consider it appropriate to define scripted
        and non-scripted content as separate markets, in view of supply and demand-side
        substitutability, as content acquirers will typically have a total budget for various
        types of content, as suppliers can easily switch their from scripted and unscripted
        product. (69)
(69)    The Notifying Party further considers that there is no need to segment by
        exhibition window nor between integration into linear or non-linear AV services,
        as this does not properly reflect commercial reality. From a supply side
        perspective, the production/supply of AV content does not differ depending on
        whether the customer is an FTA, pay-TV or SVoD operator or whether it will be
        integrated into linear or non-linear AV, and is rather offered to the highest bidding
        price. From the demand-side, content is not purchased separately for linear or non-
        linear offerings or per exhibition window. (70) Nonetheless, should the
        Commission consider a potential segmentation based on exhibition window, the
        Notifying Party submits it should only differentiate between (i) all SVoD and all
        pay-TV; (ii) FTA, (iii) PPV and (iv) TVoD. (71)
(70)    Within sports content, the Notifying Party considers that the potential
        segmentation put forward by the Liberty Media/Dorna decision (i.e. between
        regular vs. irregular and premium and non-premium sports content) can be left
        open. Regular sport events generally attract a large viewership, create traction and

        result in brand image, resulting in high licence values, but this does not exclude
        substitutability, namely considering certain irregular sports events (such as FIFA
        World Cup and the UEFA European Championship). (72) The Parties recognise
        certain difficulties regarding the definition of “premium” sports content and
        consider such distinction is subjective and not appropriate. (73) In addition,
        Notifying Party submits that football and other sports should generally be
        considered substitutable, as there is a broad range of sports that are attractive for
        media companies and viewers, and brand image is increasingly created through
        other sports than football. (74)
(71)    While considering that the exact market definition for the acquisition of sports
        content may be left open, the Notifying Party submits that the assessment of the
        Transaction should consider all these potential subsegments (i.e. regular vs.
        irregular, premium vs. non-premium, football vs. other sports). (75)
(72)    Therefore, the Notifying Party considers that it is not necessary for the
        Commission to reach a conclusion on the exact market definition as, regardless of
        how the markets are defined, the Transaction will not result in competition
        concerns.

5.1.1.3. Commission’s assessment
(73)    The Commission’s market investigation confirmed that the markets for the supply
        and acquisition of AV content should be further segmented, having confirmed
        most of the possible market segmentations in the Commission precedents (as per
        Section 5.1.1.1) as explained below.
(74)    The majority of respondents confirmed there are separate markets for
        commissioned and pre-produced AV content, and that the market should be
        further segmented by content type, e.g. between films, other AV content including
        series, reality shows, kids shows, etc and sports content. (76)
(75)    The market investigation results did not support a segmentation of the overall
        market for AV content between FTA and pay-TV rights, as the majority of
        respondents view these as part of the same market and further confirmed that the
        acquisition of broadcasting rights for SVoD services also does not constitute a
        distinct market segment. (77)
(76)    The majority of respondents who expressed a view indicated that linear and non-
        linear AV content are part of the same market, which has been confirmed both by
        the supply-side of the market (rights holders), as well as the demand-side (AV
        content buyers/competitors of the Parties). (78) Specifically, one competitor
        emphasized that “[l]inear and non-linear AV content are increasingly planned,
        marketed and consumed together. Streaming services have achieved broad

        market penetration, and many types of content – including premium and sports
        rights – are used across both linear and non-linear services. As a result, the
        boundary between these two areas has largely blurred in practical market

        linear rights exist and are referred to in the acquisition agreements, most of time
        with distinct prices applicable. However, from a business/operational
        perspective, linear and non linear rights are acquired together as there is a need
        for both of them considering our subscribers switch easily their viewing
        consumption from linear to non linear. It is simply not possible to envisage today
        an AV offer without a non linear component”. (80) This is in line with the recent
        Commission decision in Liberty Media/Dorna, in which a potential segmentation
        between linear and non-linear content was not deemed no longer relevant. (81)
(77)    In addition, while the majority of respondents who expressed a view consider a
        distinction between premium and non-premium AV content would be accurate,
        (82) some have emphasized that “what constitutes ‘premium’ is very subjective”
        and that “there is no meaningful way to distinguish content in terms of whether it
        may be categorised as premium or non-premium”. (83)
(78)    In what concerns the potential separate market for films, the vast majority of
        respondents who expressed a view consider both US and non-US films as part of
        the same product market. (84)
(79)    The market investigation confirms a segmentation between exhibition windows
        is either partially or entirely accurate but indicates, in line with the Notifying
        Party’s arguments, that both pay-TV and SVoD should be considered part of the
        same exhibition window. (85) Accordingly, the market investigation points

        rights into (i) pay-TV/SVoD – first exhibition window; (ii) pay-TV/SVoD –
        second exhibition window; (iii) TVoD; (iv) PPV; and (v) FTA.
(80)    In addition, the majority of respondents who expressed a view consider a
        segmentation between premium and non-premium films is not accurate, while
        some indicate that a distinction between recent/first run films and
        catalogue/library films would be more pertinent. (86) In what concerns a potential

        segmentation by exhibition window has been confirmed to be is either partially
        or entirely accurate, with pay-TV and SVoD being part of the same segment. (87)
(81)    In what concerns a potential separate market for other AV content, the majority
        of respondents who expressed a view confirmed a distinction between scripted
        and non-scripted content, as well a distinction by genre (e.g. general
        entertainment, reality shows, quiz shows, documentaries, etc), except in what
        concerns kids shows (as this is generally not substitutable by other content). (88)
(82)    Regarding a potential separate market for sports AV content, the market
        investigation results generally confirm the recent Commission precedent in the
        case Liberty Media/Dorna. (89) The majority of respondents who expressed a view
        confirms there are no separate markets per type of sport (e.g. motorsports, ice
        hockey, handball, etc), except in what concerns football, in which market results
        were split. While in the supply-side of the market, the majority of sports rights
        holders who expressed a view replied that there is an overall market for the
        acquisition of broadcasting rights for all sports content, other third parties
        (competitors/customers of the Parties) replied that there is a distinction between
        the acquisition of broadcasting rights for football and for other sports. (90)
(83)    In addition, the majority of respondents who expressed a view confirmed that the
        market for the acquisition of sports content could be further segmented into
        regular (i.e., occurring on a yearly/seasonal basis) and irregular sports (i.e., on a
        non-yearly basis, for instance every two years), as well as between premium and
        non-premium sports rights.(91) In this context, some respondents have nonetheless
        highlighted that “[i]n Germany, the acquisition market is commonly viewed as
        two-tiered: “premium” sports rights that are must-have subscription drivers
        (above all top-tier football like the Bundesliga and UEFA club competitions)
        versus “non-premium” or niche rights” and that “[p]remium rights are indeed
        non-substituable and can be defined according to a variety of criteria set in
        previous EC and national competition authorities decisions”. (92) A competitor
        of the Parties further explained: “In Germany, premium sports rights include, in
        particular, top-tier domestic football leagues and major international club
        competitions. Non-premium sports rights, by contrast, typically attract smaller
        audiences and play a supplementary role in programming strategies. While such
        rights can still be commercially valuable, they generally do not drive large-scale
        audience engagement or subscriptions. The economic importance of premium
        sports rights is further illustrated by the large financial investments broadcasters

(84)    With regard to a potential segmentation between FTA and SVoD/pay-TV sports
        rights, the results of the market investigation points towards such segmentation
        no longer being accurate, considering that the majority of respondents who
        expressed a view replied that FTA and pay-TV service providers/broadcasters

        technologically neutral manner, allowing bidders to exploit the rights across
        different distribution channels. In these cases, FTA broadcasters, pay-TV
        operators and streaming services may compete for the same underlying sports
        rights, particularly where rights holders prioritise maximising revenue or
        strategic reach”. (95) Another respondent highlighted that “TV operators and/or
        platforms have combined business models involving FTA and PayTV rights. This
        results in a circumstance where both FTA and paid TV providers compete for the
        same sports rights packages”. (96)
(85)    Finally, the vast majority of respondents who expressed a view confirmed that
        their replies would be the same for all German-speaking Territories. (97)

5.1.1.4. Conclusion
(86)    In light of the above, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this
        decision, it is not appropriate to consider a potential segmentation between linear
        and non-linear AV content. In what concerns all other potential segmentations,
        the Commission considers that the precise product market definition for the
        supply and acquisition of AV content may be left open, as the Transaction does
        not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market under any
        potential product market definition. However, for the purposes of this decision,
        the Commission will conduct an assessment considering each of the potential
        market segmentations presented above.

5.1.2.    Geographic market definition

5.1.2.1. Commission precedents
(87)      The Commission previously considered that each of the markets for the supply
          and acquisition of commissioned AV content and pre-produced AV content are
          either national in scope or correspond to linguistically homogenous areas.

5.1.2.2. The Notifying Party’s view
(88)      The Notifying Party considers the relevant market to correspond to linguistically
          homogenous areas, in this case, the German-speaking territories, given that rights
          holders very often do not license with reference to national territories but rather
          to a certain linguistic area. Nonetheless, the Notifying Party submits that
          geographic markets can also be left open, as the Transaction does not raise
          concerns.

5.1.2.3. Commission’s assessment
(89)      In the market investigation investigated the geographic scope of the market for
          the acquisition of AV content.
(90)      In what concerns non-sports AV content (films and other AV content), the market
          investigation was inconclusive. When asked on which geographic basis do rights
          holders license their content, the results were split between national level and
          linguistic area (i.e. German-speaking territories)”. As for the demand-side of the
          market, most competitors who expressed a view indicated they acquired licenses
          for AV content for the German speaking territories. (98) (99) The Commission
          notes that, in the case of the Parties, non-sports AV content licenses generally
          cover all German-speaking territories where the Parties are active. (100)
(91)      In the case of licenses for sports content, the market investigation did not come
          up with a clear result. An equal number of participants answered that they license
          sports AV content either at national level or for German-speaking territories.
          Taking into account that some of the participants answered that they license sport
          content on an EEA-wide or worldwide level (while the answer sub-national was
          also provided as often as EEA-wide but less often than worldwide), it can be
          concluded that, overall, more market participants license sports rights on a wider
          than national level. (101)
(92)      In this context, a competitor of the Parties explained that “[d]ue to audience reach
          and differing legal frameworks sports rights are usually licensed according to
          national markets. Sometimes, the rights for German sport events are licenced for
          German-speaking territories too.” (102) Other competitors emphasize that it
          depends on the sports right’s holder and the strategy/geographic footprint of the




(98)     For the purposes of this decision, ‘most respondents’ refers to the most selected option(s) by market
         respondents when a majority of more than 50% has not been reached.
(99)     Replies to Questionnaire “01_Licensing of AV content (Phase I)”, questions C.B.1 and replies to
         Questionnaire “04 _Wholesale and Retail TV channels/AV services (Phase 1)”, question C.B.A.1.
(100)    Form CO, Annex 9 “License Agreements Supply and Acquisition”.
(101)    Replies to Questionnaire “01_Licensing of AV content (Phase I)”, questions C.B.2 and replies to
         Questionnaire “04 _Wholesale and Retail TV channels/AV services (Phase 1)”, question C.B.A.2.
(102)    Reply of a market participant to Questionnaire “04 _Wholesale and Retail TV channels/AV services
         (Phase 1)”, question C.B.A.2.1.
                                                     19
         buyer, as licenses may also be granted on a global or multi-territorial basis. (103)
         For instance, one competitor notes that “[m]any sports rights holders continue to
         organise the sale of broadcasting rights on a strictly national basis, typically
         through separate tenders for individual countries. These territorial licensing
         structures are frequently imposed by the rights holders and do not necessarily
         reflect the commercial preferences of broadcasters”. (104) A sports right holder
         answered that licensing on a DACH-wide (the term “DACH ” refers to Germany,
         Austria and Switzerland) or German-speaking area basis would reflect
         “established market practice and the clear preference of broadcasters and
         platform operators, who typically pursue acquisition and distribution strategies
         covering the entire German-speaking area.” The market participants further
         states that “[f]rom a commercial perspective, market participants view the DACH
         region as a coherent exploitation area due to linguistic consistency, audience
         expectations, and integrated marketing strategies across Germany, Austria,
         Switzerland, Luxembourg, Liechtenstein, South Tyrol and East Belgium. In
         addition, a DACH-wide rights structure is necessary for legal and technical
         reasons, in particular to address unavoidable signal overspill across national
         borders within the German-speaking territories. Licensing on a DACH basis
         therefore ensures legal certainty, operational efficiency and alignment with the
         way demand-side market participants compete and structure their services.” (105)

5.1.2.4. Conclusion
(93)     In light of the above, the Commission concludes that, for the purposes of this
         decision, it is not appropriate to consider a potential segmentation between linear
         and non-linear AV content. In what concerns all other potential segmentations,
         the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this decision, that the precise
         product market definition for the supply and acquisition of AV content may be
         left open, as the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility
         with the internal market under any potential geographic market definition.
         However, for the purposes of this Decision, the Commission will conduct an
         assessment considering potential national markets, as well as potential sub-
         national market in the case of Trentino-Alto Adige/South Tyrol, and an overall
         market consisting of the German-speaking territories.

5.2.     Market for the wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels/AV services
(94)     TV broadcasters package AV content that they have acquired or produced in-
         house into linear TV channels, which they supply to retail TV providers. Such TV
         providers incorporate TV channels in their TV offerings to final viewers. (106)
(95)     Both Parties are active on both the demand and supply side for the wholesale of
         TV channels and AV services. RTL has minimal activities in the wholesale
         acquisition of TV channels or other AV (VoD) services (as it generally does not
         carry third-party channels or VoD services and therefore does not purchase those
         products for retail distribution) and is mainly active in the wholesale supply of TV

          channels and its VoD services (it relies on third-party distribution of its own TV
          channels). RTL provides its TV channels to satellite, cable and IPTV network
          operators. By contrast, Sky DACH is an active purchaser of pay- and FTA TV
          channels for further distribution via its platform and streaming services, whereas
          its presence in the wholesale supply of TV channels or VoD services is
          limited. (107)

5.2.1.    Product market definition

5.2.1.1. Commission precedents
(96)      The Commission previously identified a separate product market for the
          wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels, differentiating between FTA
          TV channels and pay-TV channels. (108) In addition, the Commission considered
          that the market for pay-TV channels may be further segmented into premium pay-
          TV channels and basic pay-TV channels. (109) In certain cases, the Commission
          considered that FTA TV channels might form part of the same market as basic
          pay-TV channels. (110)
(97)      Furthermore, the Commission also examined, but ultimately left open, whether
          the following additional segmentations would be appropriate: (i) by
          genre/thematic content (e.g., films, sports, news, youth and general
          entertainment), (111) (ii) between linear and non-linear services, (112) and (iii) by
          distribution technology (cable, satellite, terrestrial TV, or via the internet (referred
          to as OTT or IPTV)). (113)

                                                  21
5.2.1.2. The Notifying Party’s view
(98)     The Notifying Party submits that the relevant market is wider than just the supply
         of TV channels, and should instead amount to an overall wholesale market for the
         supply of TV channels/AV services which encompasses both linear and non-
         linear content.
(99)     Such overall market could be further segmented into (i) basic TV channels/AV
         services – this would include FTA TV channels, basic pay-TV channels and
         services and freely accessible non-linear services, and (ii) paid TV channels/AV
         services – this would include premium pay-TV channels and services and non-
         linear paid SVoD services. This distinction between basic and paid AV/TV
         channels would be warranted due to appreciable differences in content, pricing
         and carriage conditions. Another important difference between basic TV channels
         (i.e., FTA and basic pay-TV) and premium pay-TV channels is the fact that basic
         TV channels are mainly ad-financed and suppliers of basic TV channels must pay
         transmission fees to network providers for the latter to transmit the relevant TV
         signal. (114)
(100)    As regards the wholesale supply of SVoD services, the Notifying Party considers
         it appropriate to include those in the same market segment as the wholesale supply
         of premium pay-TV channels as there is no difference from the perspective of TV
         retailers. Nonetheless, the Notifying Party also recognises that SVoD services are
         generally supplied on the basis of agency agreements as opposed to the wholesale
         agreements by which premium pay-TV channels are supplied. (115)
(101)    The Notifying Party does not consider it appropriate to further segment the market
         for the wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels by genre/thematic
         content as many TV broadcasters offer channels of different genres.
(102)    The Notifying Party also submits that a segmentation by distribution technology
         would not be appropriate as the competitive conditions do not differ between the
         various technologies. Wholesale suppliers of TV channels, including RTL itself,
         offer their technologies to AV retailers irrespective of the distribution technology
         used by the latter. (116)
(103)    In any event, the Notifying Party considers that the exact product market
         definition may be left open, as the Transaction does not lead to competition
         concerns, irrespective of the exact market definition. (117)

5.2.1.3. Commission’s assessment
(104)    In the market investigation, the Commission investigated the precise scope of the
         market for the wholesale supply of TV channels.
(105)    The results of the market investigation confirmed the Commission’s precedent of
         identifying a separate market for the wholesale supply and acquisition of TV

        2021 in Case M.10475 – United Group/Wind Hellas, paras 89-90.

         channels in Germany and Austria. (118) Within such market, the majority of
         market participants that expressed a view consider the distinctions between the
         supply of FTA channels and pay-TV channels, as well as by genre, remain
         appropriate. (119)
(106)    However, the results of the market investigation yielded mixed results on whether
         a distinction should be made between basic and premium pay-TV channels, or
         whether the wholesale supply of basic pay-TV channels should rather form part
         of the market for the wholesale supply of FTA TV channels instead of premium
         pay-TV channels.
(107)    From a supply-side perspective, the majority of respondents that expressed a view
         consider that such distinction is no longer relevant in Germany or Austria. (120) In
         support of this view, one market participant for example explained that “[…] in
         Germany/DACH there is no real distinction between premium pay or basic pay,
         the German audience/customer distinguishes between paying or not paying.” (121)
         while another mentioned that “In Germany and Austria specifically, ‘basic’ pay-
         TV would in any event be considered closer to FTA than some ‘premium’ pay-TV
         channels, which also demonstrates the blurred lines between these […]
         categories. This is due to the fact that “basic pay” packages in these countries
         usually consist of FTA channels in HD-quality. The subscribers pay for the HD-
         quality, however, the channels are still ad-funded and as such identical to the
         (FTA) SD channel (except for the quality).” (122)
(108)    From a demand-side perspective, the large majority of respondents that expressed
         a view consider basic and premium pay-TV channels are complements. (123) One
         respondent highlighted the differences in content and viewer groups targeted by
         basic and premium pay-TV channels, (124) and another explained that ‘[f]or a
         competitive TV product, both basic pay-TV channels and premium Pay tv
         channels are necessary’. (125)
(109)    As regards a potential segmentation of TV channels depending on the distribution
         technology used for their transmission, a clear majority of respondents that
         expressed a view consider such segmentation no longer relevant from a supply-
         side perspective. (126) One respondent explained: “From wholesale TV service
         supplier perspective the type of infrastructure is not relevant because TV access
         products are largely interchangeable (subject to availability of certain technical

         infrastructures).” (127) Another respondent added: “Infrastructure is simply a
         means of delivery and so should not be considered as separate markets.” (128)
         According to a third respondent, “segmentation by distribution infrastructure is
         not accurate in the German market. Wholesale TV channels are generally
         supplied in identical form across cable, satellite, IPTV and OTT platforms. The
         type of delivery infrastructure does not materially affect the underlying licensing
         conditions or demand dynamics. The wholesale market operates on a
         technology‑neutral basis and is therefore not divided into separate infrastructure
         segments.” (129) And another respondent explains in simple terms: “The way of
         transportation makes no difference any more.” (130)
(110)    On the basis of the above-mentioned results of its market investigation, the
         Commission concludes that – at the level of the wholesale supply and acquisition
         of TV channels – a distinction by distribution technology is no longer relevant.
(111)    As regards a potential distinction between the wholesale supply of linear and non-
         linear rights, the vast majority of respondents that expressed a view considered
         that ancillary non-linear services are associated with TV channels to complement
         TV offering. (131) Further, none of the respondents acquiring rights to TV
         channels for retail distribution indicate that they acquire only linear or only non-
         linear rights – instead, respondents acquire both linear and non-linear rights, or
         linear rights pus ancillary non-linear rights. (132) Similarly, for companies
         supplying rights to TV channels, none of the respondents indicated that they
         supply only linear rights or only non-linear rights. Instead, respondents supply
         either both linear and non-linear rights or linear rights plus ancillary non-linear
         rights. (133) Nonetheless, the majority of respondents that expressed a view
         consider linear and non-linear rights complementary. (134) Market participants
         mainly pointed towards the different audience that can be reached and different
         view needs. For example, one market participant stated that “Generally both
         linear channels and non-linear content is important to reach an audience” (135)
         while another explained that “[…] nonlinear VoD services and linear offerings
         serve different viewer needs. Live events (sports, news, entertainment shows) are
         still consumed linearly, while VoD provides -timeflexible- usage.” (136) This was
         supported by another respondent who explained that “At present, we observe that
         linear TV channels and VoD services continue to complement each other.

         However, we expect the distinction between non‑linear services and traditional
         linear TV channels to increaslingly [sic] fade. The only material differentiation
         that remains relevant concerns whether a provider offers live events (mainly news
         and sports), as the live transmission of such events can constitute a key
         differentiating factor or unique selling proposition (USP) from the end‑user’s
         perspective.” (137) The increasing relevance of non-linear rights was also raised
         by another respondent “We believe that linear TV channels will remain relevant
         in the future but (increasingly) compelment [sic]by VoD content”. (138)
(112)    Certain respondents to the market investigation also explained that any distinction
         would depend on the specific TV channel or VoD service. For example, one
         respondent explained that “Even though streaming platforms increasingly provide
         live streams, non-linear VoD services and linear offerings serve different viewer
         needs. News broadcasts’ main intention is to inform the public about what has
         been happening recently in the here and now. Reports delving deeper into the
         current affairs of course are also produced for a live broadcast, however, can
         also be viewed further detached from the current affair event that it covers.
         International content like films and series are less dependent on any particular
         time other than that of convenience for the person viewing it.” (139)
(113)    On the basis of the abovementioned results of its market investigation, the
         Commission leaves open whether the wholesale supply and acquisition of non-
         linear AV services (including SVoD) forms part of the same market as the
         wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels, thereby amounting to an overall
         market for the wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels/AV services. (140)

5.2.1.4. Conclusion
(114)    In light of the above, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this
         decision, it is not appropriate to consider a potential segmentation of the
         wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels by distribution technology, i.e.
         by cable, satellite, DTT or via the internet (OTT or IPTV). In what concerns all
         other potential segmentations, the Commission considers that the precise product
         market definition for the wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels/AV
         content may be left open, as the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its
         compatibility with the internal market under any potential product market
         definition. This concerns in particular the question whether there is a relevant
         market for the wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels separate from the
         wholesale supply and acquisition of AV services, or rather a broader market for
         the wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels and AV services overall.
         The Commission also leaves open whether a market for the wholesale supply and
         acquisition of TV channels, should be further segmented (i) between FTA TV
         channels and pay-TV channels, (ii) between basic pay-TV and premium pay-TV
         channels, (iii) by genre/thematic content.

5.2.2.    Geographic market definition

5.2.2.1. Commission precedents
(115)     The Commission previously considered that the market for the wholesale supply
          and acquisition of TV channels is either national in scope or comprises a
          linguistically homogenous area (which can be either broader or narrower than a
          national market). (141)

5.2.2.2. The Notifying Party’s view
(116)     The Notifying Party recognises the Commission’s precedent delineating the
          market for the wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels as national in
          scope. (142) Both Parties’ agreements to supply their channels in Germany and
          Austria are at least national in scope, if not wider, and Sky DACH usually acquires
          third-party channels for Germany and Austria. (143)
(117)     Ultimately, the Notifying Party considers that the exact geographic scope of the
          market can be left open as the Transaction does not lead to competition concerns,
          irrespective of the exact scope of the market. (144)

5.2.2.3. Commission’s assessment
(118)     In the market investigation, the Commission investigated the geographic scope of
          the market for the wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels or any
          alternative market considered in Section 5.2.1.3 above. The majority of
          respondents that expressed a view in the Commission’s market investigation
          confirmed that market for the wholesale supply and acquisition of TV channels,
          including any of the potential sub-segments, is national in scope. (145)

5.2.2.4. Conclusion
(119)     In light of the above, for the purposes of this decision, the Commission considers
          that the geographic scope of the market for the wholesale supply and acquisition
          of TV channels and AV services can be left open as the Transaction does not raise
          serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market under any potential
          geographic market definition.

5.3.      Market for the retail supply of AV services
(120)     Both Parties are active in the retail supply of AV services.
(121)     RTL acts primarily as a wholesale supplier, therefore its activities as AV retailer
          are limited to its SVoD service RTL+. RTL+ offers end users access to RTL’s TV
          channels, certain live content confined to RTL+, and to a library of commissioned

          and pre-produced AV content, music, podcasts and magazines. The service is
          subscription-based and usually ad-supported. RTL offers RTL+ to customers in
          the German-speaking Territories. In the EEA, RTL+ is actively marketed in
          Germany and Austria and the (add-free) premium packages can also be booked in
          Luxembourg and Liechtenstein. As of 2026, RTL+ and HBO Max will be offered

          wholesaler and Warner will distribute the bundle as an agent. Moreover, RTL
          engages with a number of distributors as agents for the retail of RTL+, inter alia
          Amazon Prime Video Channels (146) in Germany and Austria. (147)
(122)     Sky DACH primarily pursues a DtC approach and acts as a retail supplier of
          subscription-based paid AV services in the German-speaking Territories. By
          turnover, Sky DACH’s retail business in Germany and Austria is more than […]
          times larger than its wholesale supply. Sky DACH retails its own pay-TV offering
          and SVoD offering (WOW in Germany and Sky X in Austria). The offering
          entails linear pay-TV channels, third-party linear FTA and pay-TV channels as
          well as its own and third party VoD programming, which Sky aggregates on its
          own platform for its subscribers. It uses satellite, cable, IPTV for its pay-TV
          offering and OTT for its SVoD offering. (148)

5.3.1.    Product market definition

5.3.1.1. Commission precedents
(123)     In previous cases, the Commission identified a relevant product market for the
          retail supply of AV services. On this market retail providers of AV services offer
          linear and non-linear AV services and AV content to end customers. (149)
(124)     In its past decisional practice, the Commission considered a split of the market
          for the retail supply of AV services into (i) FTA retail services and (ii) pay-TV
          retail services. (150) The Commission has also examined whether the market for
          the retail supply of AV services could be segmented further (i) between premium
          and basic pay-TV services; (151) (ii) between linear and non-linear services (pay

         Comcast/Sky, paras 57 and 59; Commission decision of 30 May 2018 in Case M.7000 – Liberty

         per view, video on demand); (152) and (iii) between distribution technologies
         (cable, satellite, terrestrial or IPTV). (153)

5.3.1.2. The Notifying Party’s view
(125)    The Notifying Party submits that there is an overall market for the retail supply
         of AV services, without the need to further distinguish between hypothetical
         subsegments such as linear and non-linear, FTA and paid AV services, pay-TV
         and SVoD, basic and premium pay, aggregated or standalone AV services, or by
         distribution technologies. In particular, the Notifying Party submits that the
         market for AV retail supply has a broad choice of suppliers, which reflects a
         strong supply-side substitutability. (154)
(126)    As regards the segmentation into linear and non-linear AV services, such
         distinction is not appropriate as end users consume linear and non-linear AV
         services in a converging manner and therefore view such services as substitutable
         (e.g., a large variety of hybrid streaming services has been emerging, which shows
         convergence of linear and non-linear AV services). Furthermore, content does not
         differ between linear and non-linear AV services. (155)
(127)    As regards the segmentation into FTA and pay-TV, the Notifying Party indicates
         that market position is interchangeable as add-supported AV offering and paid
         AV services include both linear and non-linear offerings and FTA and AV players
         are in direct competition for content rights, audiences and advertising revenues.
         Additionally, the Notifying Party notes the increasing availability of hybrid AV
         retail offers which combine the offering of a subscription-based service with
         advertising, and the ability of end users to freely switch between different tariffs.
         Due to this hybrid offerings, a differentiation between free and paid AV services
         becomes increasingly blurred. (156)
(128)    The Notifying Party also submits that the pay-TV market should also include
         SVoD services, for the same reasons explained in linear / non-linear. According
         to the Notifying Party, the same reasoning applies as, in general, linear AV
         services are (pay) TV and non-linear are VoD services. (157)
(129)    As regards the segmentation into basic and premium paid AV services, the
         Notifying Party submits that it is difficult to distinguish the content of basic pay-
         TV from the content of premium pay-TV and that offerings are broadly similar
         with only gradual differences. The growing importance of SVoD services further
         blurs the line between basic and premium paid AV services. (158)
