(130)    As regards a potential segmentation on the basis of distribution technologies, the
         Notifying Party submits that these are broadly substitutable from a consumer
         perspective (e.g., substitutable content, same basic fee for access). From the
         supply side, providers offer more than one distribution technology. Additionally,
         the distribution technologies are increasingly converging given the rapid
         development of internet infrastructures. (159)
(131)    The Notifying Party considers there is no need to define a separate market for the
         retail provision of aggregated AV offers as aggregated AV offerings differ from
         stand-alone AV offers only with respect to the number of AV services included
         in one subscription and are interchangeable from a demand-side perspective. As
         regards functionalities offered to end users, these are the same for standalone and
         aggregated AV services. Moreover, from a supply-side perspective, the barriers
         to offer aggregated retail offerings are low and switching from stand-alone to
         aggregation is possible with little effort. (160)
(132)    In any event, the Notifying Party submits that the market definition for AV retail
         services can be left open, as the Concentration does not raise serious doubts as to
         its compatibility with the internal market under any plausible market
         definition. (161)

5.3.1.3. Commission’s assessment
(133)    The vast majority of respondents to the Commission’s market investigation that
         expressed a view consider the distinction between the retail supply of FTA TV
         and the retail supply of pay-TV is still accurate. (162)
(134)    Nonetheless, some respondents submit that, although accurate, the distinction is
         increasingly blurred, e.g., one market participant sets out that “[t]he distinction
         between retail FTA TV and pay-TV remains partially accurate but is increasingly
         blurred in Germany today. Hybrid models have emerged where traditional FTA
         broadcasters offer premium subscription tiers (e.g., RTL+ Premium) whilst pay-
         TV operators incorporate advertising-supported options (e.g., WOW). The
         Commission has previously acknowledged this convergence, noting that pay-TV
         channels carry advertising and former FTA channels have introduced
         subscription fees. Both RTL and Sky now operate across both segments, making
         this binary distinction less reflective of actual competitive dynamics. The
         segmentation remains useful analytically but should be applied flexibly.” (163)
(135)    In relation to whether there is an overall market for all paid AV retail services
         (including pay TV and SVoD services), in the Commission’s market
         investigation, the majority of respondents who expressed a view considered that
         the supply of SVoD is part of the market for retail supply of pay-TV. (164) One
         respondent noted that “[f]rom the perspective of end users, the retail supply of
         SVOD services forms part of a broader market for the retail supply of pay-TV
         services. Both types of services offer access to premium audiovisual content

         as on-demand viewing in SVOD versus predominantly linear channel packages

         via SVOD services, albeit it being offered in a more user-friendly fashion, we’d
         argue that these offers are alternatives of each other to the end consumer”; (167)

         The content that is distributed via both channels is not materially different. SVoD
         providers are increasingly moving into the space of premium live events.” (168)
(136)    The majority of respondents that expressed a view consider that further
         segmentation (i) between premium pay-TV and basic pay-TV, (ii) on the basis of
         distribution technologies, and (iii) between linear and non-linear AV services, is
         no longer relevant. (169) For example, one respondent indicates that “[w]hile
         segmentation by distribution technology (cable, satellite, terrestrial, IPTV) was
         historically relevant, this distinction has diminished in importance due to the rise
         of OTT/internet-based distribution. From the consumer perspective, these
         technologies increasingly serve as substitutable means of accessing the same
         content.” (170)
(137)    The majority of the respondents consider that these considerations apply equally
         across the German-speaking Territories. (171)
(138)    During the course of the Commission’s market investigation, certain market

         whereby linear and non-linear TV/video services are available through a
         comprehensive platform with a single user interface. (172) As regards a potential
         separate market for aggregated AV services, the Commission has not previously
         considered such a market (173) and the results of the market investigation did not

          provide strong evidence in support of a separate market for such services. (174)
          Overall, the market participants seem to consider that aggregation is more a way
          to differentiate players within the same market of AV retail as opposed to
          amounting to a separate market: “[a]ggregation of content or TV channels forms
          part of existing wholesale and retail AV markets but does not constitute a distinct
          market with its own structure or pricing dynamics. Aggregated AV services
          represent a functional component of established AV service offerings rather than
          a standalone segment.” (175)
(139)     In light of the above, for the purposes of this decision, the Commission concludes
          that the questions whether (i) there should be an overall market for the retail
          supply of AV services or whether there should be separate product markets for
          FTA retail services and pay TV retail services; (ii) there should be an overall
          market for all paid AV retail services (including pay-TV and SVoD services); (iii)
          the market for the retail supply of AV services could be further segmented into
          (a) premium and basic pay pay-TV services; (b) linear and non-linear services;
          and (c) different distribution technologies; and (iv) the supply of aggregated AV
          services constitutes a separate market, can be left open as the Transaction does
          not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market under any
          possible product market definition.

5.3.2.    Geographic market definition

5.3.2.1. Commission precedents
(140)     In previous decisions the Commission considered the market for the retail supply
          of AV services to be national in scope since suppliers compete on a nation-wide
          basis to supply their AV services to end-users. (176) The Commission also found
          that the market could be based, at most, on linguistically homogeneous areas (177),
          or be limited to the coverage of a supplier’s infrastructure. (178)

5.3.2.2. The Notifying Party’s view
(141)     The Notifying Party submits that the market for the retail supply of AV services
          is at least national in scope but could also be wider by extending to either Germany
          and Austria combined or to German-speaking Territories, due to the Parties’

         approach to market in these territories does not differ and their acquisition of AV
         content usually covers these territories. (179)
(142)    Regarding the coverage area of cable operators, the Notifying Party notes that this
         geographical market can only be relevant when end customers consume AV
         services via a cable connection. (180)
(143)    In any event, the Notifying Party submits that the geographical scope for the
         market for AV retail services can be left open, as the Transaction does not raise
         serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market under any plausible
         market definition. (181)

5.3.2.3. Commission’s assessment
(144)    The vast majority of market participants that expressed a view indicated that the
         conditions of competition (including prices, consumption habits, and brands) of
         the market for the retail provision of AV services are sufficiently homogeneous
         to distinguish national markets. (182) The responses were less clear as regards
         distinguishing markets according to (German) linguistic borders or according to
         the coverage area of cable operators (for distribution by cable). (183)
(145)    In light of the above, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this
         Decision, the relevant geographic market for the retail supply of AV services and
         its segments can be left open as the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as
         to its compatibility with the internal market under any potential geographic
         market definition.

5.4.     Market for the supply of advertising space on AV content
(146)    Both RTL and Sky DACH sell advertising space on TV, SAVoD and online video
         in Germany and Austria.
(147)    TV advertising. In Germany, RTL sells advertising space on RTL’s basic TV
         channels RTL, VOX, NITRO, SUPER RTL, RTLup and ntv, as well as its
         premium pay-TV channels RTL Crime, RTL Living, RTL Passion and GEO
         Television. In Austria, RTL sells advertising space on its FTA channels RTL
         Osterreich, VOX Osterreich, NITRO Osterreich, SUPER RTL Osterreich, RTLup
         Austria and ntv Austria. RTL further sells third-party inventory for the TV
         channels Sky Sport Austria, RTLZWEI Austria, Krone.TV, Sport 1 Österreich,
         R9 Oesterreich HD and since 2024 also CANAL+ ACTION and oe24 TV.
(148)    Sky DACH mainly sells advertising space on its own premium pay-TV channels
         such as Sky Atlantic, Sky Crime, Sky Cinema Action/Family/Classics and various
         sports channels (Sky Sport Bundesliga, Sky Sport Tennis, Sky Sport Golf). While
         in Germany, SKY DACH sells some third-party TV advertising inventory, in
         Austria, all of its TV advertising sales relate to its own inventory.
(149)    SAVoD advertising. RTL sells advertising in its VoD platform RTL+. Sky
         DACH sells advertising on WOW, Sky Q, and Sky Go.

5.4.1.    Product market definition

5.4.1.1. Commission precedents
(151)     In previous decisions, the Commission considered the market for online
          advertising to be separate from offline advertising, on the basis of specificity
          (i.e. the ability to reach a more targeted audience) and pricing model. (185)
(152)     Within offline advertising, the Commission distinguished separate markets for:
          (i) the sale of advertising space on TV channels, and (ii) the sale of advertising
          in newspapers. (186) The Commission also considered a distinction between
          offline advertising sold directly by the publisher (e.g. the OTT platform or the
          TV channel) or through an intermediary but ultimately left the question
          open. (187) In Telia Company/Bonnier Broadcasting Holding, the Commission
          considered whether the sale of AVoD services is part of the market for the sale of
          advertising on TV channels but ultimately left the question open. (188) Within the
          market for the sale of advertising space on TV channels, the Commission left open
          whether further segmentation is required between the sale of advertising space on
          FTA TV channels and pay-TV channels. (189)
(153)     As regards online advertising, in Google/Fitbit and Meta/Kustomer, the
          Commission concluded that there were separate relevant product markets for the
          supply of online search advertising services on the one hand, and the supply of
          online display advertising services on the other hand, leaving open whether the
          market for online display advertising should be further segmented according to
          the platform where the ad is delivered (desktop or mobile devices), between
          video and non-video advertising, as well as between on- and off- social
          networks advertising. (190) In Google/DoubleClick, the Commission considered
          distinguishing between direct sales and intermediation for online
          advertising, with the latter potentially further segmented into search and non-

                                                 33
         search intermediation but ultimately left the market definition open. (191) Further,
         the Commission left open the existence of separate markets for direct and
         intermediated sales of online mobile advertising. (192)
(154)    Previous Commission decisions have, however, also acknowledged input from
         market participants indicating that the line between online advertising and TV
         advertising is becoming increasingly blurred due, in particular, to the rise of non-
         linear (VoD) viewing. (193)

5.4.1.2. The Notifying Party’s view
(155)    The Notifying Party considers that the distinction between offline and online
         advertising is obsolete and an overall market for the sale of advertising space in
         AV media (encompassing TV advertising, SAVoD and online video advertising)
         would be the most appropriate in light of the recent market developments and
         ongoing trends explained below. (194)
(156)    First, advertisers “follow the eyeballs” which means that they choose freely
         between TV advertising space and other advertising inventory with the purpose
         of reaching target groups wherever they are, regardless of the particular media.
(157)    Second, online video offerings are a strong substitute for TV inventory, especially
         for younger target groups. Online video consumption has grown exponentially
         over the last years, whilst TV viewership has dropped. Several customers have
         fully stopped using or at least significantly reduced their ad spend in linear TV
         advertising and opted to invest on digital advertising instead. In addition to now
         “traditional” online video (e.g. Google/YouTube or Meta), streaming services
         have also introduced ad-supported tiers (SAVoD) that allow customers to show
         ads to valuable target groups (e.g. in Germany, Netflix, Disney +, Amazon Prime
         Video, Paramount and HBO Max have launched SAVoD offerings). (195) Further,
         TV, SAVoD and YouTube are all mainly consumed on the “big screen” which
         means that even if the goal of an advertising campaign is awareness and brand
         building, advertisers don’t necessarily need to use traditional AV advertising.
(158)    Third, important advertising metrics such as reach in relevant target groups and
         cost per mile (‘CPM’) (196) tend to show a growing convergence among
         categories of AV advertising. With common measurement standards, media
         buying agencies can plan their campaign across TV and streaming. Media buying
         agencies and advertisers shift budgets across media categories and bundle cross-
         media bookings into multi-channel campaigns. (197) In addition, technological
         developments like Addressable TV advertising, linear programmatic (198) or free

(193)   Commission decision of 9 September 2014 in Case M.7288 – Viacom/Channel 5 Broadcasting,
        para. 38.
(194)   Form CO, paras 1297, 1370, 1372, 1373, 1380, 1389, 1395, 1413.
(195)   Traditional TV broadcasters are also adapting to the mentioned market changes by launching their
        own SAVoD services.
(196)   CPM means the cost of reaching 1.000 contacts. CPM is the usual “currency” for ad sales in TV
        and other media.
(197)   Cross-media contracts offer a high flexibility for shifting budgets across several media categories
        (mainly TV, addressable TV and digital, but also print and audio) throughout the year.
(198)   Programmatic linear TV is inventory in TV that is sold via adtech tools also used for online
        advertising (i.e. supply-side platforms and demand-side platforms).
                                                   34
         ad-supported streaming TV channels (199) further confirm the blurred line
         between TV and online video.
(159)    In case the Commission considers that an overall market for the sale of advertising
         space in AV media would be too broad, the Notifying Party submits that there
         should be at least a market for TV and SAVoD advertising. Companies active in
         SAVoD have already gained a strong market position based on their significant
         viewer share (Netflix and Amazon Prime Video account for 20 million users in
         Germany) shortly after their market entry and will continue to grow in the next
         years. (200)
(160)    The Notifying Party also considers that a segmentation between FTA TV and pay-
         TV advertising is artificial. For ad-funded broadcasters such as RTL, the TV
         signal distributed FTA and via paid services (cable network and IPTV operators)
         is identical. Media buying agencies and advertisers also do not (and cannot)
         distinguish between FTA TV and pay-TV in their purchases of TV inventory. (201)
         Further, the Notifying Party considers that there is no difference between direct
         and intermediated advertising sales in TV advertising (and in advertising in
         general), as media buying agencies do not choose a sales house but rather chose
         inventory across ad sales houses based on price and properties to reach campaign
         goals and contacts in the target group. (202)
(161)    The Notifying Party submits that a sub-segmentation of a market for online
         advertising into online search and online display advertising can be left open
         given that the Parties do not supply online search advertising. Further, the
         Notifying Party considers that further segmentations of the market for online
         advertising are not appropriate. With regards to a segmentation between online
         advertising on and off social networks, both formats allow for comparable
         targeting capabilities (e.g. demographic, interests, location), performance metrics
         and video formats (e.g. instream and outstream placements). On both types of
         platforms, users are exposed to online video content in a similar context (e.g.
         scrolling through social media feed or a website). With regards to the type of
         device where the ad is delivered, users engage with both desktops and mobile
         devices throughout their day and switch seamlessly between them. Media buying
         agencies plan and allocate budgets across devices based on user behaviour. With
         regards to a segmentation between direct and intermediated sales, publishers and
         intermediaries are fierce competitors with the same route to market and the same
         customers which, in turn, have the same pricing expectations towards the
         publishers/intermediaries. (203)
(162)    In any case, the Notifying Party submits that the market definition for the supply
         of advertising space in AV content can be left open as the Transaction would not
         raise competition concerns, irrespective of the exact product market
         definition. (204)


(199)   Free ad-supported streaming TV (‘FAST’) channel is a streaming service that provides AV
        programming over the internet. It resembles content-wise traditional broadcast television as it is
        free to watch, follows a fixed programming and is funded by advertisements. From a technical
        perspective, it is a streaming service.

5.4.1.3. Commission’s assessment
(163)    Overall, the market investigation indicated that the approach taken by the
         Commission in previous cases with regard to definition of the market for the sale
         of advertising space is in most aspects still currently accurate, but the market
         dynamics continue to evolve. (205)
(164)    The majority of respondents who expressed a view considered that the distinction
         between online and offline advertising is currently accurate. (206) Respondents
         explained that the line between online and offline advertising is increasingly
         blurred but there are currently still significant differences. (207) For example, one
         customer indicated that “there are still different pricing mechanisms and reach
         potentials”. (208) Another customer explained: “from a media planning and
         buying perspective, this fundamental distinction remains accurate and relevant in
         the German market. The two channels still differ significantly in their core
         planning metrics, pricing mechanisms (e.g., GRP-based vs. auction-based
         CPM/CPC), and measurement frameworks. However, it is crucial to note that the
         lines are blurring, primarily due to the rise of Connected TV (CTV) and
         Addressable TV (ATV), which merge traditional TV environments with digital,
         data-driven advertising methods. While the core segmentation is still valid for
         strategic purposes, the market is clearly evolving towards a more hybrid
         ecosystem”. (209)
(165)    The vast majority of respondents who expressed a view also considered that the
         distinction between the sale of linear TV advertising space (210) and the sale of
         advertising space on other forms of media channels (such as billboards,
         newspapers, radio, etc.) is still currently accurate. (211) For example, one customer
         explained that “linear TV offers a unique combination of high reach, audiovisual
         impact and simultaneous mass audience delivery, which is not replicated by other
         offline media. From a commercial perspective, linear TV advertising is typically
         planned and bought primarily for branding and broad reach objectives, using
         GRPs and audience-based pricing. Other offline media are generally used for
         different communication purposes, rely on different measurement approaches and
         are not considered close substitutes for linear TV by advertisers”. (212)
(166)    The vast majority of market respondents who expressed a view in Germany
         considered that the segmentation of linear TV advertising between FTA TV and

         linear channels (and vice versa). Sales houses for pay-TV channels and for FTA

         such content”. (215) However, most respondents who expressed a view in Austria

         “FTA TV channels typically offer broad reach and a diverse programme mix (e.g.
         news, general entertainment and public service content), which makes them
         particularly suitable for large-scale advertising campaigns aimed at reaching
         wide audiences. In contrast, Pay TV channels generally have smaller and more
         specialised audiences and tend to focus on specific types of content (such as
         movies or sports). As a result, from an advertiser’s perspective these two types of
         channels may serve different advertising objectives.” (217)
(167)    With regards to online advertising, the vast majority of market respondents who
         expressed a view considered that the distinction between online search
         advertising and online display advertising is currently accurate. (218) Further,
         the vast majority of market respondents who expressed a view considered that,
         within online display advertising, the segmentation between the supply of video
         and non-video advertising is currently accurate. (219) One competitor highlighted

(169)    With regards to SAVoD (225) or AVoD (226) advertising, the majority of market
         respondents who expressed a view considered that these are complements and not

         Respondents often mentioned that SAVoD and AVoD advertising are used to
         reach younger target audiences.
(170)    In relation to SAVoD advertising in particular, competitors stated that “SAVoD is
         currently not a full substitute (alternative), but rather a necessary complement to
         compensate for the reach losses of linear TV among younger target groups” (227)
         and “linear TV channels (both FTA & Pay) still reach slightly different target
         groups then SAVoD. Especially target groups older 55y are using much more
         Linear TV vs rest of population and on Linear TV advertisers can reach them in
         a more cost-effective way”. (228) Similarly, customers indicated that “we consider
         SAVOD as a complement of linear TV for specific younger target groups we do
         not reach anymore on linear TV only” (229) and “we consider SAVoD advertising
         a complement to Linear TV in Germany. While Linear TV, including channels

         profile – primarily individuals interested in sports and online betting – which are
         crucial for both our brand building and acquisition campaigns. They offer a
         distinct engagement opportunity rather than being direct substitutes for Linear

5.4.1.4. Conclusion
(177)     In light of the above, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this
          decision, it can be left open whether there is a relevant market for TV advertising
          only (including whether it should be further segmented between FTA TV and
          Pay-TV advertising) or whether SAVoD and/or online video advertising on
          YouTube should be included in the same market as TV advertising since the
          Transaction does not give rise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the
          internal market even under the narrowest potential product market definition
          (market for the supply of pay-TV or FTA within a separate market for linear TV
          advertising only, or market for the supply of SAVoD advertising on mobile or
          desktop devices). For the same reason, the question of whether the market for
          offline or online advertising should be segmented between direct and
          intermediated sales can also be left open.

5.4.2.    Geographic market definition

5.4.2.1. Commission precedents
(178)     In its previous decision, the Commission considered that the market for the supply
          of advertising space on TV channels (and potential segments thereof) is national
          in scope. (248)
(179)     As regards the market for the supply of online advertising space (and potential
          segments thereof), the Commission previously defined the market as national in
          scope or alongside linguistic borders within the EEA. (249)

5.4.2.2. The Notifying Party’s view
(180)     The Notifying Party does not oppose the Commission’s conclusions in its past
          decisions. (250)

5.4.2.3. Commission’s assessment
(181)    The market investigation confirmed that the geographic scope of the market for
         the supply of AV advertising space is national in scope.
(182)    The vast majority of market participants who expressed a view purchase and/or
         supply advertising space on TV channels, VoD platforms or online, in Germany
         or Austria, at a national level. (251)

5.4.2.4. Conclusion
(183)    The Commission considers that, for the purposes of this Decision, the exact
         geographic scope of the market for the supply of AV advertising space can be left
         open as the Transaction does not give rise serious doubts as to its compatibility
         with the internal market under any potential geographic market definition. In this
         Decision, the Commission will conduct an assessment on the potential national
         markets (Germany) or linguistic borders (Germany + Austria combined). (252)


6.       COMPETITIVE ASSESSMENT
(184)    Under Article 2(2) and (3) of the Merger Regulation, the Commission must assess
         whether a proposed concentration would significantly impede effective
         competition in the internal market or in a substantial part of it, in particular
         through the creation or strengthening of a dominant position.
(185)    In this respect, a merger may entail horizontal and/or non-horizontal (namely,
         vertical or conglomerate) effects. Horizontal effects are those deriving from a
         concentration where the undertakings concerned are actual or potential
         competitors of each other in one or more of the relevant markets concerned.
         Vertical effects are those deriving from a concentration where the undertakings
         concerned are active on different or multiple levels of the supply chain.
         Conglomerate effects are those deriving from a concentration where the
         undertakings concerned are in a relationship which is neither horizontal nor
         vertical. A concentration may involve all three types of effects. In such a case, the
         Commission will appraise horizontal and non-horizontal effects in accordance
         with the guidance set out in the relevant notices, that is to say the Horizontal
         Merger Guidelines (253) and the Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines (254).
(186)    In assessing the competitive effects of a merger, the Commission compares the
         competitive conditions that would result from the notified merger with the
         conditions that would have prevailed without the merger. In most cases the
         competitive conditions existing at the time of the merger constitute the relevant
         comparison for evaluating the effects of a merger. However, in some

(251)   Replies to Questionnaire “02_Advertising - Germany (Phase 1)”, questions D.C.1, D.C.2 and
        D.C.3.; replies to Questionnaire “03_Advertising - Austria (Phase 1)”, questions D.C.1, D.C.2 and
        D.C.3.
(252)   The Commission will assess the markets for the supply of AV advertising space which are affected
        in Germany+Austria (combined) as an approximation of the German-speaking region because the
        Parties are only active in Germany and Austria and therefore could only provide market shares of
        these two countries combined. However, the Commission noted that the provided market shares
        overstate the Parties’ market positions in a German-speaking region.
(253)   Guidelines on the assessment of horizontal mergers under the Council Regulation on the control of
        concentrations between undertakings (’Horizontal Merger Guidelines’), OJ C31, 5.2.2004, p. 5.
(254)   Guidelines on the assessment of non-horizontal mergers under the Council Regulation on the control
        of concentrations between undertakings (‘Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines’), OJ C 265,
        18.10.2008.
                                                   42
          circumstances, the Commission may take into account future changes to the
          market that can reasonably be predicted. (255)

6.1.      Horizontal effects

6.1.1.    Legal framework
(187)     The Horizontal Merger Guidelines describe two main ways in which horizontal
          mergers may significantly impede effective competition. In particular, the
          proposed concentration might be creating or strengthening a dominant position:
          (i) by eliminating important competitive constraints on one or more firms, which
          consequently would have increased market power, without resorting to
          coordinated behaviour (non-coordinated effects); and (ii) by changing the nature
          of competition in such a way that firms that previously were not coordinating their
          behaviour, are significantly more likely to coordinate and raise prices or otherwise
          harm effective competition (coordinated effects). (256)

6.1.1.1. Non-coordinated effects
(188)     A merger giving rise to horizontal non-coordinated effects might significantly
          impede effective competition by creating or strengthening the dominant position
          of a single firm, one which, typically, would have an appreciably larger market
          share than the next competitor post-merger. Moreover, also mergers that do not
          lead to the creation or the strengthening of a single firm’s dominant position may
          create competition concerns under the substantive test set out in Article 2(2) and
          Article 2(3) of the Merger Regulation. Regarding mergers in oligopolistic
          markets, the Merger Regulation clarifies that “under certain circumstances,
          concentrations involving the elimination of important competitive constraints that
          the merging parties exerted upon each other, as well as a reduction of competitive
          pressure on the remaining competitors, may, even in the absence of a likelihood
          of coordination between the members of the oligopoly, result in a significant
          impediment to effective competition”. (257)
(189)     The Horizontal Merger Guidelines list a number of factors which may influence
          whether or not significant horizontal non-coordinated effects are likely to result
          from a merger, such as the large market shares of the merging firms, the fact that
          the merging firms are close competitors, the limited possibilities for customers to
          switch suppliers, or the fact that the merger would eliminate an important
          competitive force. Not all those factors need to be present to make significant
          non-coordinated effects likely and it is not an exhaustive list. (258)
(190)     The Horizontal Merger Guidelines acknowledge that a merger that creates or
          strengthens the market power of a buyer may also significantly impede effective
          competition, in particular by creating or strengthening a dominant position. In
          certain circumstances, competition in the downstream markets could be adversely
          affected if, in particular, the merged entity were likely to use its buyer power vis-
          à-vis its suppliers to foreclose its rivals. (259)



(255)    Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 9; Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 20.
(256)    Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 22.

         Merger Guidelines.
(258)    Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 26-36.
(259)    Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 61.
                                                  43
(191)    To assess whether a merger that creates or strengthens buyer power would
         significantly impede effective competition, the Commission must analyse the
         competitive conditions in the upstream markets and to evaluate the possible
         positive and negative effects of the merger. (260)
(192)    In this context, paragraphs 24-36 of the Horizontal Merger Guidelines apply
         mutatis mutandis to the buyer side of the market, to investigate whether the
         Transaction would result in an increase in buyer power, as a result of the
         elimination of important competitive constraints that the merging Parties exerted
         upon each other prior to the Transaction.

6.1.1.2. Coordinated effects
(193)    A merger in a concentrated market may also significantly impede effective
         competition due to horizontal coordinated effects where, through the creation or
         the strengthening of a collective dominant position, it increases the likelihood that
         firms are able to coordinate their behaviour and raise prices, even without entering
         into an agreement or resorting to a concerted practice within the meaning of
         Article 101 TFEU. A merger may also make coordination easier, more stable or
         more effective for firms that were already coordinating before the merger, either
         by making the coordination more robust or by permitting to coordinate on even
         higher prices. (261)
(194)    To assess whether a merger gives rise to horizontal coordinated effects, the
         Commission should examine, firstly, whether it would be possible to reach terms
         of coordination and, secondly, whether the coordination would likely be
         sustainable.
(195)    As regards the possibility of reaching terms of coordination, coordination is more
         likely to emerge in markets where it is relatively simple to reach a common
         understanding on the terms of coordination. Coordination may take various forms,
         including keeping prices above the competitive level, or dividing the market, for
         instance by customer characteristics or by allocating contracts in bidding
         markets. (262)
(196)    As regards the sustainability of coordination, three conditions are necessary for
         coordination to be sustainable. Firstly, the coordinating firms must be able to
         monitor to a sufficient degree whether the terms of coordination are being adhered
         to. Secondly, discipline requires that there is a credible deterrent mechanism that
         can be activated if deviation is detected. Thirdly, the reactions of outsiders, such
         as current and future competitors not participating in the coordination, as well as
         customers, should not be able to jeopardise the results expected from the
         coordination. (263)
(197)    Moreover, in examining the possibility and sustainability of coordination, the
         Commission should specifically consider the changes that the Transaction brings
         about. The reduction in the number of firms in a market may in itself be a factor
         that facilitates coordination.
(198)    In assessing the likelihood of coordinated effects, the Commission should take
         into account all available relevant information on the characteristics of the

(268)     With regards o geographic allocation of revenues, in what concerns the market for the acquisition of non-sports AV Content, the Notifying Party submits that the national level
          estimates were broken down from the Parties' estimates to the overall German-speaking territories, at it is market standard that non-sports content is licensed for the overall
          German-speaking territories. The Parties therefore allocated both their own figures and the estimates derived for competitors across the hypothetical national markets in proportion
          to each country's household share, subject to certain national-level refinements. As a result, the national-level shares largely mirror the shares for the German-speaking territories
          (Methodology Note: AV Content, 26 January 2026, Section 3.2). For sports content, the market shares for each hypothetical national market include (i) all licenses that only
          relate to the national market in question, and (ii) a share of all multi-territorial licences that cover the national market in question. Specifically, the total value of multi-territorial
          licenses was generally allocated to the countries within German-speaking territory based on the number of German-speaking households in each country (Methodology Note:
          AV Content, 26 January 2026, Section 3.1.2).

6.1.3.2. Overall AV Content and non-sports AV content

        6.1.3.2.1.       Germany

6.1.3.2.1.1.    Notifying Party’s views
(210)    The Notifying Party submits that the combination of the Parties’ activities does
         not pose a risk to effective competition in the overall market for the acquisition
         of pre-produced AV content (sports and non-sports) in Germany. (269) In
         addition, the combination of the Parties’ activities also does not pose a risk to
         effective competition in the market for acquisition of pre-produced non-sports
         content, in any of the affected potential segments of (i.e. films, US films, non-
         scripted other AV content, SVoD/pay non-sports content, and 1st window – other
         AV content, in Germany. (270)
(211)    The Notifying Party submits that the overall market for the acquisition of AV
         content, as well as the market for non-sports AV content (and potential
         subsegmentations thereof) are characterized by several competitors (such as
         P7S1, Netflix, Amazon, ARD and ZDF) and the presence of global streaming
         players, who will likely maintain high competitive pressure in the market. The
         Transaction would not impact the competitive landscape. (271)
(212)    The Notifying Party expects the market volume for pre-produced AV content to
         increase in the following years. Production studios, such as Disney, which had
         limited their supply in the past years, focusing on their own streaming services,
         but have started expanding their licensing activities again. (272)
(213)    Accordingly, in addition to increased supply and market volume, the demand for
         pre-produced AV content is also expected to increase. The market shift towards
         non-linear AV content consumption has been driving an increase in overall
         demand for pre-produced content, as many broadcasters aim to remain relevant
         by launching streaming services as well (e.g., P7S1 with Joyn). (273)
(214)    The Notifying Party further submits that acquirers of pre-produced AV content
         face a few large production studios, such as Warner, Universal, Paramount,
         Disney, Amazon and Sony, which cover around over [80-90]% of the supply
         market and have significant bargaining power. Many of these sellers are also
         vertically integrated, having their own means of AV distribution and will not
         depend on sales to the merged entity post-transaction. (274)
(215)    In addition, the Notifying Party submits the Parties are not close competitors in
         any of potential subsegments for non-sports AV Content, specifically films, US-
         films, non-scripted other AV content, SVoD/pay-TV non-sports content and other
         AV content – 1st exhibition window, because RTL, as a primarily FTA



(269)   Form CO, paras. 263, 418 and 630.
(270)   Form CO, para. 563.
(271)   Form CO, paras 426 and 564.
(272)   Form CO, paras 422 and 564. Disney has focused almost entirely on its own streaming service and,
        in particular, very much limited the licensing of pre-produced films and series to the wider market.
        The exploitation of Disney’s films in FTA has only been possible in very small, tightly tailored
        packages and within short timeframes. This restrictive strategy has led to a shortage of AV content
        on the market.
(273)   Form CO, paras 421, 426, 424 and 564.
(274)   Form CO, paras 427 and 564
                                                    66
         broadcaster, and Sky DACH, as a pay-TV operator, acquire licenses with different
         focuses, due to distinct audience and brand positioning. (275)
(216)    The Notifying Party also submits that the Transaction does not pose competitive
         concerns in the potential market for acquisition of commissioned AV content, in
         which the only affected potential market segment in Germany is the acquisition
         of commissioned non-scripted other AV, in the genre reality shows. Sky
         DACH’s activities in the acquisition of commissioned AV content, and in such a
         hypothetical market segment, are negligible and there are other strong competitors
         are active in this segment, especially global streamers, such as Netflix and
         Amazon have a strong business in commissioned content (so called “Originals”)
         and are expected to increase their spendings here. Additionally, different genres
         of scripted or non-scripted commissioned content are highly interchangeable. (276)

6.1.3.2.1.2.    Commission assessment
(217)    The Commission considers the Transaction is unlikely to lead to an increase in
         the merged entity’s bargaining power vis-à-vis its supplier (buyer power) that
         would be liable to result in a significant impediment of effective competition in
         the overall market for the acquisition of pre-produced AV content. The
         Commission further considers that the Transaction is unlikely to lead to an
         increase in the merged entity’s bargaining power vis-à-vis its supplier (buyer
         power), which would result in a significant impediment of effective competition
         in the potential market segments for the acquisition of non-sports AV content,
         namely the acquisition of pre-produced SVoD/pay-TV non-sports content, pre-
         produced films, pre-produced US films, pre-produced non-scripted other AV
         content, pre-produced other AV content – 1st exhibition window and
         commissioned non-scripted other AV content – reality shows.
(218)    First, the Commission notes that Transaction gives rise to moderate combined
         market shares. In what concerns the overall market for the for acquisition of pre-
         produced content, the Transaction gives rise to combined a market share of around
         [20-30]%, with small increments of [5-10%] coming from RTL, as per Table 1
         above.
(219)    In relation to the acquisition of pre-produced non-sports AV content, the
         Transaction gives rise to combined market shares below [20-30]% in all affected
         potential market segments, as per Table 2 above. In addition, other purchasers,
         such as P7S1, Netflix and Amazon, have comparable market shares to those of
         the Parties, or even holding higher market shares in some potential segments, for
         instance in SVoD/pay-TV non-sports content (Netflix: [30-40]% and Amazon:
         [20-30]%), other AV content – 1st exhibition window (Amazon: [40-50]% and
         Netlfix: [20-30]%).
(220)    In what concerns the potential segment for commissioned non-scripted reality
         shows, where the combined share reaches [40-50]%, the increment coming from
         Sky DACH is minimal ([0-5]%). The HHI delta in this market amounts to 151,
         which is one point above the threshold of 150 to consider this an affected market
         and is therefore deemed insusceptible to lead to an increase in buyer power, in
         exceptional circumstances such as those in the present case.
(221)    Second, the Commission considers that the Parties are not close competitors in
         the overall market for pre-produced AV content acquisition of pre-produced

(275)   Form CO, para. 565.
(276)   Form CO, para. 260.
                                            67
         content, and in any of the potential segments for the acquisition of non-sports AV
         content.
(222)    In the first place, the Parties have different strategy focuses due to their distinct
         business models (FTA vs. pay-TV), audience reach and demographics. As a
         mainstream broadcaster, RTL focuses on broad reach and mass appeal and is well
         known for inhouse captive production and commissioned productions. Therefore,
         licensing of pre-produced AV content serves as a complement in linear TV, being
         more important to its streaming service RTL+. In any case, RTL focus more on
         local productions. Sky DACH, by contrast, focuses on premium content, with a
         strong sports component, for a paying audience. This difference is reflected in the
         limited overlap between their respective audiences. (277)
(223)    In the second place, the market investigation confirmed that the Parties are
         generally not considered to be close competitors. The majority of the respondents
         who provided a view confirmed that Netflix and Amazon are the closest
         competitors to the Parties in all affected potential market segments, except in the
         case of commissioned non-scripted reality shows, where the main competitors are
         RTL and P7S1. (278)
(224)    In the third place, the Commission considers that even if each of the affected
         potential segments were to be considered separate markets, they would
         nonetheless face strong out-of-market constraints by other segments. In general,
         broadcasters/AV service providers aim to have a varied AV content portfolio to
         appeal to their audience, as has been confirmed by the results of the market
         investigation, in which the majority of competitors of the Parties replied that they
         have an overall budget for the licensing of AV content (regardless of the type)
         and replied they aim for a mix of sports and non-sports AV content, including
         films and other AV content. (279) This can also been verified by the evolution of
         streaming services, who provide an increasingly wider range of AV content, not
         only limited to pre-produced films and series, but also include commissioned
         content, reality TV, documentaries, as well as sports events (see also Section
         6.1.3.3).
(225)    Third, the overall results of market investigation do not support a finding that
         the transaction would lead to increased buyer power of the merged entity,
         resulting in a significant impediment of effective competition.
(226)    In the first place, while the majority of respondents who expressed a view consider
         the Parties are important buyers in each of the affected potential segments for the
         acquisition of pre-produced non-sports AV content, the market investigation also
         confirmed that there will be increased competitive pressure from global streaming
         platforms (such as Netflix, Amazon, etc) in the future. (280)
(227)    In the second place, the majority of rights holders who expressed a view expect
         that the bargaining power of the Parties will remain similar in the overall market


(277)   Form CO, paras.279, 428 and 1163.

        and replies to Questionnaire “04 _Wholesale and Retail TV channels/AV services (Phase 1)”,
        question D.A.A.A.C.1 and D.A.A.A.C.2.
(279)   Replies to Questionnaire “04 _Wholesale and Retail TV channels/AV services (Phase 1)”, questions
        D.A.A.A.A.2 and D.A.A.A.A.3.
(280)   Replies to Questionnaire “01_Licensing of AV content (Phase I)”, questions D.B.A.2 and D.B.A.5.
        and replies to Questionnaire “04 _Wholesale and Retail TV channels/AV services (Phase 1)”,
        question D.A.A.B.A.2 and D.A.A.B.A.3.
                                                  68
         for the acquisition of pre-produced AV content. (281) In relation to the potential
         affected segment related to non-sports AV content, (282) while the majority of
         respondents indicated a possible increase in the Parties bargaining power post-
         Transaction, the Commission considers such increase to be consistent with fact
         that the pre-Transaction market shares of the Parties were similar-sized and
         amounted to circa [10-20]% each, (283) therefore leading to a more relevant market
         position of the merged entity. However, such increase in bargaining power would
         not lead to competition concerns, considering the overall competitive assessment
         described herein.
(228)    The results of the market investigation with regards to the affected potential
         segment for commissioned non-scripted reality shows is also consistent with the
         minimal increment coming from Sky DACH, as the majority of rights holders
         indicated the bargaining power will remain similar. (284)
(229)    In the fourth place, most competitors indicated that negotiations for the license of
         non-sports rights are balanced, except in the case of the acquisition of pre-
         produced films, for which most competitors consider rights holders have the
         leverage on price. (285) In the case of the overall market for the acquisition of pre-
         produced AV content, the results of the market investigation were inconclusive
         as to who holds the leverage on licensing negotiations, and several respondents
         indicated this would depend significantly on the type of content negotiated. As
         highlighted by one competitor “the acquisition of AV content depends on the
         degree of competition regarding the specific content. Sometimes purchasers have
         more leverage on price and rights, sometimes rights holders.” (286)
(230)    In the fifth place, the merged entity will continue to face competitive constraints
         from several players, given that the majority of respondents (both rights holders
         and competitors) confirmed the existence of sufficient alternatives in the overall
         market for the acquisition of pre-produced AV content and each of the affected
         potential segments related to non-sports rights. (287) In this context, one
         competitor summarized that “[s]ince there is a high demand for content and a
         diverse need in rights, there are sufficient alternative purchasers to the
         Parties.” (288)
(231)    In addition, the majority of respondents who expressed a view confirmed the
         existence of low barriers to entry in the overall market for the acquisition of pre-
         produced AV content, as well as in the affected potential market segments for the
         acquisition of non-sports AV content, as well as recent market entry, except in the

         case of the potential segment of commissioned non-scripted reality shows, for
         which the results on recent entry have been inconclusive. (289)
(232)    In the case of the affected potential segment for commissioned non-scripted
         reality show, the Commission notes streaming platforms (such as Netflix,
         Disney+ and Amazon Prime Video) have been expanding their portfolio of this
         segment, having increased their spend on reality shows. (290)
(233)    Finally, the majority of respondents who expressed a view indicated they consider
         the transaction will have a neutral impact on their business, which is consistent
         with the views described above regarding the existence of sufficient alternatives,
         lack of significant barriers to entry and recent entry by new market players. (291)
(234)    Fourth, while the Commission acknowledges negative views expressed by two
         non-sports rights holders in the context of the market investigation, which
         essentially point to the fact that there would be one less buyer for smaller
         independent producers. (292) However, the Commission notes that the overall
         results of its market investigation do not support the conclusion that the
         transaction would lead to a significant impediment of effective competition in the
         affected potential market segments for the acquisition of non-sports rights, as
         presented above.
(235)    In addition, other rights holders have also provided positive views on the
         Transaction, emphasizing, for instance, that:
         (a)        “We expect that the overall competitive environment for audiovisual
                    content distribution in Germany will remain dynamic, with multiple
                    broadcasters and streaming platforms active in the market.” (293)
         (b)        “This merger could be an opportunity for [rights holder] to increase our
                    business since in the last years most of the business was done with RTL
                    and not with SKY.” (294)
(236)    Accordingly, the Commission considers that the Transaction will not lead to an
         increase in buyer power by the merged entity, resulting in a significant
         impediment to effective competition in the Germany.

6.1.3.2.1.3.    Conclusion
(237)    In light of the above, and for the purposes of this Decision, the Commission
         concludes that the Transaction does not give rise to serious doubts as to the
         compatibility of the Transaction with the internal market as a result of horizontal
         unilateral effects in the overall market for the acquisition of pre-produced AV
         content, as well as any potential segments for the acquisition of non-sports rights

        6.1.3.2.2.      German-speaking Territories

6.1.3.2.2.1.    Notifying Party’s views
(238)    The Notifying Party’s assessment is the same for the potential market of overall
         German-speaking Territories as for the potential national market for Germany, as
         AV content rights are often acquired in multi-territorial bundles covering the
         whole of the German-speaking Territories. Therefore, the arguments presented in
         Section 6.1.3.2.1.1, namely existence of a multitude of players on the demand-
         side, bargaining power of licensors, continued competitive pressure of global
         streamers, as well as the Parties not being close competitors, are applicable to the
         overall German-speaking Territories. (295)

6.1.3.2.2.2.    Commission assessment
(239)    The Commission considers the Transaction is unlikely to lead to an increase in
         the merged entity’s bargaining power vis-à-vis its supplier (buyer power) that
         would be liable to result in a significant impediment of effective competition in
         the overall market for the acquisition of pre-produced AV content, and in any
         potential market segment for the acquisition of non-sports AV content in the
         German-speaking Territories (i.e. pre-produced SVoD/pay-TV non-sports
         content, pre-produced films, pre-produced US films and pre-produced other AV
         content – 1st exhibition window).
(240)    First, the Commission notes that the Transaction gives rise to combined market
         shares, at most around and usually, below [20-30]% in all affected potential
         market segments, as per Table 4 and Table 5 above. In addition, the market shares
         evidence there is market fragmentation, as there are other purchasers such as
         P7S1, Netflix and Amazon with comparable market shares to those of the Parties
         and holding higher market shares in the potential segments, for instance in
         SVoD/pay-TV non-sports content (Netflix: [30-40]% and Amazon: [20-30]%),
         other AV content – 1st exhibition window (Amazon: [40-50]% and Netflix:
         [20-30]%)
(241)    Second, considering the Transaction gives rise to the same affected market
         segments as in Germany (with the exceptions of pre-produced non-scripted other
         AV content and commissioned non-scripted other AV content, reality shows), the
         Commission’s assessment with regards to Germany, set forth in
         Section 6.1.3.2.1.2 above, namely in what concerns the Parties’ not being close
         competitors, the results of the market investigation, in which the majority of
         respondents confirmed their replies would be the same for all German-speaking
         Territories, (296) the existence of a competitive market with a multitude of players
         and the high competitive pressure from global streaming platforms, is also
         applicable to the German-speaking Territories.

6.1.3.2.2.3.    Conclusion
(243)    In light of the above, and for the purposes of this Decision, the Commission
         concludes that the Transaction does not give rise to serious doubts as to the
         compatibility of the Transaction with the internal market as a result of horizontal
         unilateral effects in the overall market for the acquisition of pre-produced AV
         content, and in any potential market segment for the acquisition of non-sports AV

6.1.3.3. Sports AV content

        6.1.3.3.1.      Germany

6.1.3.3.1.1.    Notifying Party’s views
(275)    The Notifying Party submits the combination of the Parties’ activities does not
         pose a risk to effective competition in an overall segment for sports content in
         Germany. (309)
(276)    While Sky DACH has a more sizable market position, RTL is only a minor player
         in most market segments and the Parties are not close competitors, in any of the
         affected potential market segments. Sky DACH’s main value proposition is a
         strong offering of sports content. It therefore typically bids for larger packages,
         whereas RTL typically bids for smaller packages to complement its portfolio as a
         mainstream broadcaster. (310)
(277)    The Notifying Party submits that the sale and purchase of sports rights in
         Germany (as well as for the overall German-speaking Territories), especially the
         most important sports rights, is done highly competitive tender procedures,
         organized by sophisticated licensors, who design them to maximise competition
         and revenue, and who enjoy strong market positions. (311) A relatively large
         number of high-level sports events provides steady stream of tenders or bilateral
         sales processes for premium sports content. (312)
(278)    The market for sports content is dynamic with a multitude of different players
         competing for the content. As market entry is open to all potential bidders that
         enter a tender procedure there is a relatively large cluster of competitors that are
         not fully homogenous. In addition to core sports providers like DAZN, the
         publicly funded broadcasters, network operators such as DTAG, global streaming
         services such as Netflix, Amazon Prime, Paramount+, Disney+, Warner HBO,
         and Apple TV intensify their activities on the market continuously. (313)
(279)    As for football rights, RTL’s loss of the UEFA Europa League and Europa
         Conference League (in November 2025), for the season 2027-2031, for DAZN,
         will result in a significant decrease of RTL’s market share in this potential
         segment. In addition, the vast majority of football rights are awarded through
         tender procedures ensuring a competitive environment by nature and global
         streamers are increasingly focus on football rights.
(280)    With regards to regular non-premium sports content, the Notifying Party
         submits that a dynamic market exists and will continue to intensify, with a wide
         variety of sports for relatively low license costs, making the market accessible and
         attractive for all players to enter, switch or expand their activities, as has been the
         case for global streamers. (314) In addition, the Notifying Party submits that many
         licensors also enjoy very strong market positions, similar to those of premium
         sports holders, for instance popular global franchises like Formula 1, NBA and
         NFL. Increasing demand for sports content also further strengthens the rights
         holders bargaining power.


(309)   Form CO, para. 568.
(310)   Form CO, paras 270, 467 to 468, 568 and 570.
(311)   Form CO, paras 469, 470, 568 and 571.
(312)   Form CO, paras 469, 526, and 578.
(313)   Form CO, para 478.
(314)   Form CO, paras 530.
                                                 77
(281)    The Notifying Party also submits that an analysis of Parties’ bidding behaviour
         showed that the parties generally did not bid for the same licenses (in 150 licenses
         bids, there was only [information on the Parties’ bidding behaviour], thus
         confirming the Parties are not close competitors in the regular non-premium
         sports segment. (315)
(282)    Due to regular re-negotiation or tender procedures of sports rights, the Parties’
         market share is merely a snapshot of currently concluded rights sales only and
         may shift quickly in the near future. In fact, the RTL’s higher market share in
         regular non-premium sports is mostly related to UEFA Europa
         League/Conference League rights, which have been lost to DAZN in the recent
         tender. (316)

6.1.3.3.1.2.    Commission assessment
(283)    The Commission considers the Transaction is unlikely to lead to an increase of
         in the merged entity’s bargaining power vis-à-vis its suppliers (buyer power)
         that would be liable to result in a significant impediment of effective competition
         in the market for the acquisition of sports AV content, any potentially affected
         acquisition market segments in Germany (i.e., acquisition of commission and pre-
         produced sports content, acquisition of pre-produced sports content, football,
         other sports and regular non-premium sports). (317)
(284)    First, the Commission notes that the Transaction gives rise to small market share
         increments in all affected potential market segments, except for regular non-
         premium rights. While the Parties have a combined market shares above 30% in
         each of the six affected potential markets in Germany, and above 40% in regular
         non-premium sports ([40-50]%), the increment coming from RTL is around
         [5-10]% or lower for all of these segments, except for regular non-premium
         sports, which amounts to [20-30]%, with similar-sized shares for both Parties
         (Table 3 above).

         UEFA Europa League and Conference League, despite having significant viewer
         appeal, are not considered to fall within the category of premium sports because

(287)    Second, the Commission considers the Parties are not close competitors in the

         with RTL’s market share does not lead to an affected market ([0-5]%), and the
         potential market segment of football ([30-40]%), in which RTL achieves only
         [5-10]% share. RTL’s highest market share relates to the potential market segment
         of regular non-premium sports ([20-30]%), in which Sky DACH’s market
         position is more equally sized ([20-30]%). (323) Nonetheless, the Commission

         essentially unchanged post-transaction, and the transaction is unlikely to lead to

(294)    Third, the overall results of market investigation do not support a finding that
         the transaction would lead to increased buyer power of the merged entity,
         resulting in a significant impediment of effective competition.
(295)    In the first place, the majority of respondents who expressed a view consider that
         RTL and Sky DACH are each important buyers of sports content in each affected
         potential segment in Germany, but indicated that sports rights holders have
         leverage in negotiations, specifically in relation to the price (license fees), as well
         as other license conditions, in each potential market segment. This is only not the
         case for regular non-premium sports, in which most respondents who expressed a
         view considered negotiations to be balanced between licensors and licensees. (330)
         For instance, one competitor emphasises that “[t]here is always only 1 rights
         holder per competition, whereas there are multiple potential buyers, creating a
         sellers market” and further explains that “for non premium, long-tail sports there
         are fewer buyers and many different leagues/competitions created a balanced
         negotiation position”. (331)
(296)    In the second place, when asked about the Parties’ bargaining power post-
         Transaction, the majority of sports rights’ holders who expressed a view indicated
         that it would remain unchanged. (332) Although this view is not shared by the
         majority competitors, who consider the Parties bargaining power will increase
         post-Transaction, (333) the Commission considers that sports rights holders, in
         their capacity of licensors, are in the best position to assess the effect of the
         Transaction on the bargaining power of the merged entity in the market for the
         acquisition of sports AV content. (334)
(297)    In the third place, the market investigation results confirmed the Parties’ argument
         that there are several players in the market for the acquisition of sports AV
         content. The majority of respondents who expressed a view, both sports rights
         holders as well as other third parties (competitors/customers of the Parties)
         indicated that there are sufficient alternative purchasers to the Parties in each
         sports market segments. (335)
(298)    In the fourth place, while the majority of sports rights holders replied that there
         are no significant barriers to entry in the market for the acquisition of sports rights,
         the majority of competitors of the Parties who expressed a view replied the

         opposite, having identified barriers to entry, essentially related to the high costs
         of some sports rights, and highly regulated tender procedures. (336)
(299)    The barriers to entry identified by competitors mostly relate to the acquisition of
         football or premium sports rights (which would include, at least, the Bundesliga
         and the UEFA Champions League), which are generally associated with higher
         license fee costs, as well as structured formal tender procedures. (337) The
         Commission notes that regulatory barriers relating to tender procedures of the
         most relevant rights are essentially designed to ensure competition, on the one
         hand, and guarantee FTA broadcasting of certain sports content considered of
         public interest, on the other, in some cases with the involvement of national
         competition authorities. (338) For instance, the FCO introduced the “no-single-
         buyer rule” in the 2017-2021 Bundesliga cycle and, thereby, ensured continued
         “fragmentation” of live coverage and created space for new entries. (339)
(300)    Therefore, the existence of high costs for the acquisition of such sports rights most
         generally relates to the strong position of rights’ holders in relation to price, as
         well as other conditions of the license, as has been confirmed by the market
         investigation. The fact that there are several market players bidding for the
         acquisition of sports rights is also a driver of higher fees, as high demand leads to
         increased prices due to the exclusive nature of sports rights. Market entrants’
         ability to successfully acquire sports AV content usually depends on their
         financial ability to make the most attractive commercial offer. This has been
         confirmed by the market investigation, as the majority of sports rights holders
         indicated that price is the main criterion for selecting a purchaser. (340)
(301)    In addition, the lack of significant barriers to entry in the market for the acquisition
         of sports AV content is also supported by recent entry, not only by global
         streaming platforms but also from smaller local players. This was the case of Dyn,
         a German-based company founded in 2022, which was launched as a SVoD
         service in August 2023, providing sports content related to handball, basketball,
         volleyball, table tennis and hockey. (341)
(302)    Accordingly, the Commission considers that the high barriers identified by
         competitors in the market investigation are mostly linked to strong leverage and
         the competitive nature of the market for sports rights and would therefore not
         support a finding that the Transaction is likely to strengthen buyer power by the
         merged entity.
(303)    In addition, the majority of respondents who expressed a view confirmed the
         Parties’ argument that there has been recent entry (in the last 2 years) of new

(305)    In the sixth place, the majority of respondents who expressed a view consider that
         the Transaction will have a neutral impact on their business and either a positive
         or a neutral impact in the market for the acquisition of sports content. (345)
         Concerns were not raised by sport rights holders, except for one respondent, active
         in the segment of other sports and regular non-premium sports, who emphasized
         that currently RTL and Sky compete to acquire similar packages of the rights

         in one less buyer in the market. The Commission considers this isolated concern
         on the side of sports rights holders is insufficient to raise serious doubts regarding
         the Transaction, considering the overall assessment presented herein.
(306)    Fourth, the Commission considers there is evidence of a relatively dynamic
         market environment for the acquisition of sports, characterized by the limited
         duration for which sports rights are tendered and that different players may win
         such rights each time there is a new tender. In addition, there has been recent entry
         of new players and increasing competitive pressure from global streaming
         platforms, who are expanding demand across sports content, including football,
         basketball and tennis.
(307)    In the first place, there are various players competing for the acquisition of sports
         content, as live sports constitute an important driver for subscriptions. (346) In
         addition to core sports providers like DAZN, the publicly funded broadcasters,
         network operators such as DTAG, and global streaming platforms are
         increasingly active, there has also been recent entry by some local players, such
         as DYN and Sport 1. (347)
(308)    In the second place, there is evidence of new entrants having successfully
         challenged strong market positions in the past. For instance, DAZN entered the
         market about 10 years ago and is currently the second most important competitor
         in terms of market share in most of the affected markets/segments (i.e., acquisition

(309)    Moreover, Dyn, who has entered the market in August 2023, with a focus on the

(310)    In the third place, global streamers have entered the market for the acquisition of
         sports content and are increasing their presence, a trend which is expected to
         continue in the future, as confirmed by the market investigation result (presented

         League rights by Paramount +: (353)
         (a)       Amazon had previously acquired rights for the UEFA Champions
                   League (€95 million) and the Wimbledon (€5 million), as of season

                   Champions League (first pick package for Wednesday) and NBA rights
                   as of 2027;
         (b)       Disney+ entered the European market for sports content, including
                   Germany (and other German-speaking Territories), with the purchase of

                   Christmas Day from 2024 to 2026;
         (d)       Major League Soccer is globally exclusively available on AppleTV+;
         (e)       HBO Max acquired live content to the Olympics winter 2026.
(311)    These recent changes in the market will likely have a non-negligible impact on
         market shares and therefore on the competitive pressure on the merged entity.
         According to the Notifying Party’s estimates for the overall market for the

(312)    The Commission considers that this dynamic creates high competitive constraints
         on the merged entity, as global streamers are now entering or further expanding
         their portfolio in sports content but have already and broad and established
         consumer base, as well as strong financial capacity . This means global streamers
         are able to significantly increase their investment in sports, in order to maintain
         and/or diversify its customer base. Accordingly, any potential increase in the
         bargaining position of the merged entity post-Transaction would face significant
         constraints by these global market players and is therefore unlikely to result in a
         significant impediment to effective competition. This is in line with the stated
         rationale of the transaction, which is to gain scale in order to be able to better
         compete with these global market players, as explained in Section 2.2.
(313)    Fifth, specifically in what concerns the potential segment of regular non-
         premium sports, the Commission also considers there is a competitive market
         with a multitude of players.
(314)    In the first place, there is a wide variety of non-premium sports content and
         relatively low license costs, making such market attractive for new players to
         enter or expand their activities. This is due to the large number of different events
         and related rights, providing a steady stream of new rights to the market. Further,
         new sports, namely US sports (such as NFL) are being promoted and becoming
         more popular in the EU. (355)
(315)    In the second place, there are several alternative purchasers, as confirmed by the
         market investigation results presented above, including ARD, ZDF, P7S1, Sport1,
         DAZN, DTAG (Magenta), Amazon, Dyn, DF1, Paramount+ (new entrant as of
         2027), Disney+ (new entrant as of 2025), YouTube, Netflix and HBO.
(316)    In addition to global streamers, who have been acquiring both premium and non-
         premium sports rights (as described above), local players are also investing in this
         segment. For instance, P7S1 acquired rights to the FIFA Club World Cup 2025,
         Ice hockey World Championship and WWE wrestling. Recently, in golf, Sky
         DACH lost its rights to PGA Tour and PGA DP World Tour to DTAG. Dyn
         acquired the rights for the Handball-Bundesliga and likely the upcoming EHF
         Champions League European League and European Cup. (356)
(317)    The Commission also notes that the expansion of global streaming services in the
         premium segments, will also lead to a shift of budget of some acquirers that get
         outbid into the non-premium segments (“trickle-down effect”), increasing
         competition for non-premium rights and thus increasing the pressure for
         competitive bids also for the merged entity. This was already the case for DAZN,
         who previously held the rights to the UEFA Champions League and lost the bid
